ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U6	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Two months ago, on 17 April, the Chairman of the Constitutional Committee Rait Maruste handed over to the Riigikogu the draft law on partnership initiated by 40 members of the Riigikogu, which will arrive at your first reading today. Rait Maruse began his speech by passing on this draft with the following words: 'Estonia is a democratic rule of law founded on freedom in accordance with our constitution, where everyone is equal before the law, everyone's rights and freedoms are respected and no one is to be discriminated against. ' Those Rait Marust words are today, two months later, perhaps more topical than ever, more topical than I could have imagined. This bill has become a real test of tolerance, liberality, but also of honesty for politicians and everyone interested in it. I call on all the debaters on this draft to be, above all, honest today and in the future. Let us be honest and talk about what this bill contains and what it changes. Let it be said from the beginning once again to everyone who has not understood it or has not wanted to understand it: the Cohabitation Act does not legalise the same-sex marriage in Estonia and does not give the same-sex couples the right to adopt. I say once again, very slowly and with a loud and clear voice: the Cohabitation Act does not legalise the same-sex marriage in Estonia and does not give the same-sex couples the right to adopt. The assertions that this bill is somehow undermining our constitution or endangering the family institution are unfounded. This draft concerns all those who have chosen a common-law relationship as a form of partnership. It is estimated that 170,000 of these people are in Estonia, over 99% of whom are heterosexual couples who for some reason have not wanted to get married or have not been able to do so. Some hundreds of couples are of the same sex, who currently do not have the opportunity to obtain any minimum legal protection from the state for their relations in Estonia. However, coming back to the words of Rait Marust, the Republic of Estonia is a democratic rule of law founded on freedom in accordance with the Constitution, where everyone is equal before the law, everyone's rights and freedoms are respected and no one is discriminated against. This bill has been accused of being carried out hastily and over the top. Let us also look at this statement and take a brief look at the process that has brought us here today to the first reading of this draft. Over the last few years, the Ministry of Justice has been actively involved in seeking an answer to the question of whether and how the legal consequences of non-marital cohabitation, including cohabitation of same-sex couples, should be regulated. The aim of the Ministry of Justice is to ensure adequate legal protection for non-marital partners in such a way as not to undermine the institution of marriage or reduce the motivation of couples to enter into marriage. As a first major step, the Ministry of Justice published an analysis of non-marital cohabitation and its legal regulation in the summer of 2009, five years ago, and asked the public address published that year for proposals and reflections on whether and how future regulation of non-marital cohabitation should be developed. Many authorities and persons submitted opinions and suggestions on the analysis prepared by the Ministry of Justice. In addition, in his letter of 23 May 2011 to the Minister of Justice, the Chancellor of Justice considered that the legal framework in force did not sufficiently guarantee the protection of the rights of the partners in fact and that it was therefore necessary to supplement the legal regulation in this respect. Among other things, the Chancellor of Justice took the view that the permanent cohabitation of same-sex persons falls within the scope of the fundamental right of the family, which is why the Constitution precludes a situation in which such cohabitation is not legally regulated. On 1 August 2011, the Ministry of Justice proposed four options for the political parties in the new Parliament to regulate non-marital cohabitation and asked for their views on this. Responses were received from all four parliamentary parties. According to these, three quarters of the political parties represented in Parliament consider that the issue should be addressed, and one quarter considers the current situation to be satisfactory. There is therefore political readiness to deal with legal issues arising from non-marital relationships at law level. An interparty working group was formed in the Riigikogu in the spring of this year, which together with the Ministry of Justice put together a draft text. The draft Cohabitation Act sets out a comprehensive gender-neutral regulation covering the legal relationships between persons who have concluded a partnership agreement and legal relations with third parties. From the analysis to date, it can be concluded that the legal consequences of non-marriage partnerships, including same-sex couples, need to be regulated. The analysis revealed that a large number of couples live in non-marital partnerships in Estonia, and the majority of children are born extramaritally. Today, 60% of children are born in Estonia outside of marriage, which is why we are clear leaders in the European Union. Such developments have led to the need to protect the weaker side of the partnership. In addition, the issue of giving couples of the same sex a simpler and more flexible way to regulate their cohabitation is becoming more and more acute. There are real legal problems in the relationship between non-marital couples. The sharpest question is mostly the property acquired during the life of a partnership, including the most problematic common housing. Similarly, life partners have no mutual maintenance obligations under the applicable law and one after another can only inherit if they have a will or a succession agreement. In response to the letters sent to the public, a number of problem situations were identified where the current law does not offer well-functioning solutions. For example, difficulties in applying for housing loans, problems in dividing assets acquired jointly in the event of separation, problems in determining and inheriting the number of beneficiaries of social benefits, banning hospital visits, inability to give consent to medical procedures and problems in the exercise of parental rights. People living in non-marital partnerships, regardless of gender, are exposed to these problems. The Cohabitation Act includes a flexible package of options to address the most common legal problems. In addition, the termination of the partnership agreement is accompanied by a simplified procedure for the transition from the partnership agreement to the marriage in order to motivate persons of different sexes to enter into a partnership agreement in order to protect their rights. At present, the legal protection of non-marital people requires better than average legal knowledge and therefore lacks the entrepreneurial spirit and the incentive to regulate their cohabitation legally. However, the conclusions of the forthcoming proposals pointed out that the possibility of registering partnerships for special-sex couples was not strictly necessary. Indeed, the parties are somewhat protected by the regulation of the partnership agreement and there is an opportunity to enter into a marriage. First of all, the function of the Cohabitation Act could be to protect their rights in situations where marriage is not recommended or is not yet ready for it. The Cohabitation Act allows for a transitional period for special-sex persons when the rights and obligations of the parties are less extensive. For example, the partner's maintenance obligation is limited to each other, as well as the parties' choice as to whether the maintenance obligation will continue to apply after the termination of the contract. However, it also helps to motivate partners of different sexes to regulate their legal relationships. The current legal framework requires the parties to be active and have strong legal knowledge, for example, in order to be guided by the regulation of the partnership agreement, which, with its complexity, may seem unfathomable to the parties. The regulation of the Cohabitation Act that a notary shall draw up a contract for the parties, ensuring that the interests of the incompetent party are not undermined, and the package brought together in the law to resolve the common legal problems encourages the parties to regulate their partnership legally. Similarly, the simplified rules of the Cohabitation Act in the case of a transition from a partnership agreement to a marriage will ensure that the parties, if they do not wish to do so, do not have to pre-divide property or reach the same arrangements for the second time in the case of a marriage, once they have already agreed upon them at the time of the conclusion of the cohabitation agreement. The conclusions of the forthcoming proposals pointed out that the linking of certain social guarantees with the registration of partnerships could have a positive impact on the motivation of couples of different sexes. In doing so, the stakeholders pointed out that, if people of different sexes were allowed to regulate their cohabitation, this would help to solve the current problems of determining the number of beneficiaries of social benefits, such as the cases of soldiers who have died. The Cohabitation Act offers a complex gender-neutral standard solution to the most common legal problems that a person may face in a cohabitation. In conclusion, the conclusion of the partnership agreement is as follows: Persons wishing to enter into a contract shall contact the notary for this purpose. It examines whether the conditions for the conclusion of a partnership agreement are met and there are no circumstances preventing the conclusion of the contract. Notar's control obligation is specifically stated in the Act. Notar, convinced that the conditions for the conclusion of the contract have been fulfilled and that there are no grounds to exclude the conclusion, will start to draw up a contract with the persons wishing to conclude the partnership agreement. Notar explains the different options to the parties, including the issues that the parties must agree on (e.g. choice of property) as well as the issues referred to in the Cohabitation Act, which the parties can also agree (post-life maintenance obligation, mutual will). Both parties wishing to conclude a partnership agreement are present at the same time as the notary and express in person a declaration of intent which may not be conditional on their wish to enter into a partnership agreement. The rights and obligations of registered partners during the partnership agreement can be summarised as follows: The rights and obligations of registered partners are governed by the Partnership Agreement and by the Law, including the applicable consequences which the partners have further regulated by mutual agreement in the Partnership Agreement. A person who has concluded a partnership agreement cannot enter into a marriage at the same time; the only exception is to conclude a marriage between partners at the same time as the partnership agreement is concluded but with the conclusion of the marriage. The requirement of notarising the conclusion of a partnership agreement ensures that the partners understand their rights and obligations. The maintenance obligation provided for in the partnership agreement, which covers both property and operational contributions, helps to ensure the protection of the weaker party and ensures flexibility for the parties in the organisation of the partnership. Entries in the register shall also ensure the visibility of the differences arising from the choice of partners' property relationships to third parties. The options for ending the partnership agreement are summarised below: The partnership agreement ends when the partner dies, the partners conclude a marriage or the partnership agreement is terminated. In the event of the death of a registered partner, the termination of the contract shall be the moment of the death of the partner and the moment of the marriage of the partners. The partnership agreement can be terminated by agreement of the parties at the notary or in the event of a dispute before the court. A specific case is the termination of the partnership agreement when the partners conclude the marriage between themselves. Then agreements and transactions may end, but the Cohabitation Act provides for the possibility that legal relationships will continue, which will give people of different sexes the opportunity to transform the partnership agreement into a "simplified" marriage. The regulation of the European Union does not oblige Estonia to adopt the gender-neutral regulation of the partnership agreement. At the same time, attention must be paid to developments in other Member States of the European Union and the equal treatment of same-sex and special-sex partners, also in the light of the recent European Court of Justice ruling. In the Member States of the European Union, the institution of marriage is also open to partners of the same sex in eight countries: France, the Netherlands, Belgium, Denmark, the United Kingdom, Spain, Sweden and Portugal. Same-sex partners can register partnerships in 21 countries: Austria, Belgium, the Czech Republic, Andorra, Iceland, Denmark, Germany, Finland, Hungary, Ireland, Luxembourg, Malta, the Netherlands, Liechtenstein, Slovenia, Sweden, Great Britain, Norway, France, Switzerland and Portugal. This list included other countries, not just those of the European Union. Many countries increasingly rely on the idea that the laws should remain as neutral as possible in relation to different forms of partnership and moral treatment. People should be able to develop close relationships according to their desires and preferences by identifying the ethical foundations of their family life. However, the role of family law could be limited to providing solutions to practical problems, and assessments of forms of relationships should be avoided. I have been told, or have rather been warned, that I am committing political suicide defending this bill. If that's the case, fine. I will at least do this by being honest with myself and defending a bill that I sincerely believe in in. I have come to this on the basis of my experience in life and experience in traveling around the world. I, too, have once been a true Soviet man who was afraid of the most new and strange. I've been racist, homophobe, and generally intolerant to everything else. My transformation began in 1982 when I fled to Sweden and experienced in my skin how helpful and friendly a society can actually view a stranger, a foreign concern. From that moment on, my transformation began, but it didn't happen overnight. But here today I am prepared to commit political suicide to make Estonian society become a little more friendly, tolerant and open. Thank you. [[Let's hear it.]]

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U85	No further questions. Thank you very much, Valdo Randpere. For a co-report, please address the Chair of the Committee on Legal Affairs, Neeme Suur.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U86	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, As rapporteur for the Committee on Legal Affairs, I am talking about the procedure that took place in the Commission. The Committee on Legal Affairs dealt with the draft law 650 in four debates. The first debate took place on Monday 9 June, the second on Tuesday 10 June, the third on Thursday 12 June and the fourth on Monday 16 June. The first discussion was attended by Neeme Suur, Marko Pomerants, Ken-Marti Vaher, Peeter Võsa, Igor Gräzin, Kalle Jents, Imre Sooäär and Valdo Randpere. Andres Anvelt, Minister of Justice, and Susann Mikli, Adviser to the Department of Private Law, were invited. The Minister reported on the position of the Government of the Republic. At the second debate, on Tuesday 10 June, the members of the Committee on Legal Affairs and Olga Sõtnik took part as an alternate member. Procedural decisions were taken. It was decided to put the draft on the plenary agenda by 17 June (5 votes for, 3 against and 1 abstention, 1 member of the Commission not taking part in the vote). Neeme the Great (5 votes for, 4 against and 1 abstention) was appointed rapporteur. When the first reading of the draft is closed, the Commission's proposal to set the deadline for the submission of amendments at 12 September (5 votes in favour and 3 abstentions, 2 Commission members did not take part in the vote). A longer discussion took place during the Commission's third debate, Thursday 12 June. There were few members of the Committee on Legal Affairs this time, including Neeme Suur, Marko Pomerants, Jüri Morozov and Valdo Randpere, while there was a quorum. Indrek Niklus, Head of the Department of Private Law of the Ministry of Justice, and Susann Mikli, Adviser to the same department, answered the questions at the time. The fourth treatment of the draft decision took place on Monday 16 June. There was no discussion at the time, but the Commission's position on the draft was adopted. A proposal was put forward to reject the draft at first reading. The Commission did not support this proposal. The votes were split in half: 5 for and 5 against. So much for dealing with the bill. Now I'm talking about the discussion that took place. At the first debate, on Monday, 9 June, the initiator Valdo Randpere presented the draft to us. There was the Minister of Justice who presented the position of the Government of the Republic, which, in a very brief summary, can be said to be supported by the Government. The Minister admitted that there was a lot of work to be done with the draft, 87 laws and 64 regulations needed to be revised, but confirmed that the Ministry of Justice had started reviewing the accompanying legislation and was prepared to contribute to the improvement of the draft in cooperation with the Ministry of Interior, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Chamber of Notaries. There were a lot of questions. For example, they asked what the difference was between a partnership agreement and a marriage agreement. The originator explained that the difference is within the objectives of these types of contracts. The association agreement is intended to serve a specific purpose, and the partnership agreement regulates civil relations in a long-term permanent partnership. It was a question that, if the partnership agreement is a normal civil law contract, why, according to the draft, should it not be concluded while away, why must both parties be present? A representative of the Ministry of Justice explained that this is a contract with a very wide-ranging impact that creates long-term relationships, including property relationships, mutual maintenance and maintenance obligations, etc. In such cases, it is appropriate to require or assume the presence of both parties. There was an interest in not concluding a partnership agreement with relatives. A representative of the Ministry of Justice explained that relations with relatives have already been governed by family law and other laws, which would be a cover for regulations that could create confusion. To avoid this, a partnership agreement with relatives is excluded. There was a question of how protection for the weaker party would develop. The initiators of the draft act explained the main areas of regulation and the fact that protection of the weaker party does not automatically arise according to the draft act, but instead comes from the creation of a new form of contract which provides an alternative way of formalising the relationship between them. It requires a declaration of will. So there will be no automatic protection of the weaker party under the partnership agreement. The question was also about who initiated the draft. From the last waitress's answers, we heard it. It was a long story. It was pointed out that according to § 4 of the bill, the notary has an obligation to establish where the person actually lives. They asked whether such an obligation was reasonable and whether it could be done at all. The answer of the representatives of the Ministry of Justice was that notaries still have a duty to establish their actual place of residence in several proceedings. This is "conventional practice," and this is nothing special. It should be noted, of course, that the House of Notaries and the Ministry of the Interior were of the opinion that the residence of a person in the population register should be taken into account rather than his actual residence. There was also the question of the difference between the legal knowledge needed to conclude a partnership agreement or a partnership agreement with a notary. The explanatory memorandum to the draft law states that it is easier to conclude a partnership agreement than a partnership agreement. The representative of the initiators of the draft act explained that the partnership agreement is a package of different rights and obligations and is therefore easier to conclude. For example, the association agreement does not establish successions or mutual maintenance relationships; the partnership agreement is a simplified package of different legal relationships that accompany a long-term partnership. There was also the question of what is the prognosis of the effects of the draft, the expected impact, for example, on people living in a common-law relationship. The initiators' response was that it was difficult to predict the effects in Estonia, but in Sweden, for example, the decrease in the number of regular marriages has stopped. During the Monday debate, the members of the Commission found that the time for asking and answering questions was short. It was requested to continue the debate at the Thursday session, and so was the case. There was no substantive debate during the Tuesday debate, the circumstances of the draft procedure were discussed. It was clarified that procedural decisions had to be taken in any event. The procedural decisions relating to the rapporteur on the draft were taken, the first reading in plenary and the deadline for the submission of amendments should the first reading be closed. It was decided that the debate would take place on Thursday. There was a debate on Thursday. Indrek Niklus and Susann Mikli from the Ministry of Justice and Marko Pomerants, Neeme Suur, Valdo Randpere and Jüri Morozov from the Legal Affairs Committee were present. A number of questions were also asked back then. They asked if child protection would arise in the event of adoption under § 16. The answer of the representative of the Ministry of Justice was that there would be an additional right and also an additional right for the partners. Family life is an argument for adoption. Account will be taken of the relationship with the child, there will be the possibility to apply for joint custody and the right to communicate. The question was whether the partners could have the opportunity to submit a joint income tax return. This question received a positive answer so that it could be. Reference was made to the judgments of the European Court of Justice, according to which the partnership should not be discriminatory in relation to the benefits laid down by the State in relation to the matrimonial relationship. Statistics were asked about how many people this bill actually covers. Statistics show that 548286 people live in Estonia in partnership with a partner, including 378026 people in partnership with a legal spouse, whereas 169832 people live in a common-law relationship. This is where they asked if there were 170000 people or a couple. That's 170,000 people after all. There are 428 people living in a common-law relationship whose partner is of the same sex. These are the data from the last census. It was a question of what legal relationships the law on partnership then covers as a package. The answer was that the Cohabitation Act includes mutual due diligence, assistance and maintenance obligations, precludes marriage during the term of the contract, confers a joint and several right on the partners and gives them joint and several liability in the transactions, in certain cases gives them responsibility for the obligations assumed by the other partner, gives them the concept of common housing, gives the partner the right to communicate with the child and the right to adopt, gives him the right to inherit and gives him the opportunity to agree on property relationships. It was also a question of the similarities and differences between life and marriage. As an example, minors can get married, and minors cannot sign a partnership agreement. Once again, questions were asked about the differences between the partnership agreement and the partnership agreement. It was reiterated that the partnership agreement is a joint operation towards a common goal, but not a civil relationship arising in a long-term partnership. It was discussed whether proof of a factual cohabitation could in any way be stated in the draft or how the status of a factual cohabitation could be stated in family law. The representatives of the Ministry of Justice explained that there are several problems. First of all, it is not possible for third parties to know about the actual partnership, i.e. it is not visible to third parties, without proof being given to third parties in specific cases. Another problem is that the beginning and end of a factual partnership are very difficult to explain and prove. Thirdly, taking into account the reality of living together can violate people's private autonomy. If people want to live without registering a relationship, the so-called third party should not decide whether or not there was a partnership. It's still a two-man decision. So much for discussion. There was no discussion on Monday, June 16. The representatives of the Ministry of Justice were invited, but since it was proposed not to involve them in the debate, because it was thought that the questioning in the Commission would not take us forward, the representatives of the Ministry did not come to the meeting on Monday. There was a vote on the Commission's position. The Commission's position not to support the proposal to withdraw the draft means that the Commission did not take a clear position. According to the comments of the Riigikogu's Home and Rules of Procedure Act, the Commission's position in this case is considered rather favourable, but in fact we did not reach such an understanding in the Commission. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U181	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, First of all, I want to assure you that we in the IRL accept the right of all people to live in exactly the way they see fit, unless it is dangerous for others. The law of cohabitation has justified a great deal of passion, and the debate has been very emotional. This is true for many reasons, both in substance and in form. Personally, I am most disturbed by the lying and wrestling of some of the initiators of the bill. So far, it is not possible to understand who the real author of this draft is. Already on 19 March, the day before the rainbow government had signed the coalition agreement, Mr Imre Sooäär sought support signatures for the draft Cohabitation Act. In an article published a few days later, however, he mentioned that the final text of the draft text was not yet complete. He expressed his hope that the bill would be ready in the coming weeks. Dannar Leitmaa, published in the Estonian Express on 27 March, said 'Cohabitation Act, a bill no one wants to talk about', complains Imre Sooäär that he has somehow accidentally become the advocate of the bill and, in fact, the committee is led by his colleague Rait Maruse. Maruse, in turn, says that he supports a free society, the right of people to regulate their living together in this society, but what exactly is contained in the bill is not his intention to say either. 'This project, however, has been written by the Ministry of Justice, and they know more about it,' he says. The minister's spokesperson, in turn, claims that the draft is initiated by the Riigikogu Commission and the Ministry cannot comment on this. Unfortunately, I am not aware of the existence of such an official Riigikogu working group. It is also impossible to identify the formation of a working group, meetings, discussions or the results thereof. It is unclear why so much is required to be confused when it is claimed that the draft is necessary. Why, then, does no one want to honestly say that the real purpose of this draft is to give homosexual relations legal recognition and social acceptance and to legitimise the so-called homosexual marriage in a hidden way? Why can't we have an honest discussion on this? Why is it necessary for some initiators to say that the draft concerns 170000 people or to stress the obvious unfounded assertion that regulating relations between same-sex people at the level of the law does not concern others? Attorney-at-Law Ilmar-Erik Aavakivi has also made his opinion very clear on this subject. Have real impact assessments been carried out in Estonia? In its letter of 29 May 2014 to the Committee on Legal Affairs, the Chamber of Notaries considers it worrying that the solution chosen in the draft proposal for regulating cohabitation does not meet the objectives set out in the draft explanatory memorandum: 'We consider that the draft does not fulfil its main stated objective, as the property relationships between couples living in a factual partnership (but not registered) do not change anything and that the problems identified in the explanatory memorandum and by the Chancellor of Justice remain the same. However, regulation serves another purpose: to give couples of the same sex the possibility to register their cohabitation in such a way that it is also visible to third parties. We believe that the draft gives the impression that giving couples of the same sex an opportunity to register together would be the main objective of the law, which is to hide behind the legal problems arising from the factual cohabitation of (specialised) couples and is not in line with good legislative practice. ' Is it acceptable, in a democratic rule of law, to use legislative power to realise any hidden objectives, even if this results in over-regulation of the legal system? The problems of unregistered people living together were already discussed in 2007, when the Ministry of Justice, which was then led by Rein Lang, developed a draft family law. The explanatory memorandum to the draft law analyses the aspects of common-law relationship: 'The fact that different forms of cohabitation are not regulated in the Family Act does not mean that there is no regulation in Estonian law to define the legal relationships between common-law relationship and same-sex partners and that people's rights and interests remain unprotected. The problem is that partners, although aware that the family law is not applicable, do nothing to determine their property relationships before they break up. It is also often believed that if the rights and obligations of partners are not regulated by the Family Law, no other law will. However, this is not the case. In particular, in order to avoid further disputes, the partners themselves will be able to determine and acquire more valuable items (housing, car) jointly in their contractual relations. If there is only a break-up to explain legal relationships, it will be difficult to reach a mutually satisfactory solution, both in the event of marriage and in the event of the breakdown of a partnership. ' I would like to address another important aspect of this issue, namely homosexual marriages. The highest court in the European Union ruled in December 2013 that in Member States of the European Union where homosexual marriage is not legalised, civil partnerships must grant equal rights to same-sex couples. On 12 December, the Ministry of Justice confirmed that the decision of the Supreme Court of the European Union does not affect the legal order of Estonia, according to which in those Member States where same-sex marriage is not legalised, couples of the same sex must be granted equal rights in civil partnerships. The press representative of the Ministry of Justice told Postimees that the case only applies to situations where people who are married are treated differently and those who have entered into a civil partnership. According to Estonian law, it is not possible for persons of the same or different sexes to enter into a civil partnership with each other, which would be comparable to the civil partnership which the European Court of Justice, on the basis of French law, examined in that case. Thus, the case referred to does not affect Estonia's legal order, he said. I hope that all the initiators have understood how much these issues are related and how much this bill concerns marriage as an institution. We in the IRL don't think it's right or necessary to legitimize gay marriages. We know that in many Western countries the granting of legal recognition to the homo partnership has been a step in the attempt to legitimise so-called homosexual marriages. If there is a real desire in our society to legally redefine the meaning of marriage and family, then it must be discussed honestly and directly, rather than manipulating and vaulting, as has already become a day-to-day decision in the draft procedure, and a referendum. Since this is a decision of such principle, on which there are conflicting opinions in society, it is not acceptable to divide society with one-sided decisions that ignore the beliefs of many people. Otherwise, we risk a very large part of society becoming alienated from political power. If we want to act as real democrats, we must listen to the voice of the people and put the law on partnership in a referendum. I would also like to remind you, good ambassadors, that according to the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia, as representatives of the state power, we have a constitutional obligation to protect the family institution as a basis for the survival and growth of the people and for society. Thus, the Constitution also obliges us to protect the family from ideological attacks and attempts to redefine it. For the above reasons, the IRL Group proposes to reject the draft at first reading.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U192	Thank you. Are guests allowed to clap on the balcony and interrupt the hearing like some kind of theatrical performance?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U193	Thank you very much. It seems to me that the people who were on the balcony joined those who were in the hall. [[Interjections.]] We're not going to have a discussion with the guests during the hearing. I would ask Eldar Efendiev to introduce at first reading the draft law amending the Citizenship Act initiated by the Group of the Estonian Centre Party.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U194	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Members of the Riigikogu! You have before you the draft law amending the Citizenship Act 645. Let's start with the first reading of this bill. As stated in the explanatory memorandum, on the one hand, the need to bring the Citizenship Act into line with the conventions ratified by Estonia and the internationally recognised principle of giving citizenship to all children, on the other hand, led to the need to overcome the conflict on the ground of national relations. The draft provides for the exemption of applicants for citizenship over 65 years of age from the examination of Estonian language and the possibility for them to pass the examination of knowledge of the Constitution and Citizenship Act of the Republic of Estonia in Estonian, English or Russian. The United Nations Committee on the Rights of the Child and the Consultative Committee of the Framework Convention on the Protection of National Minorities of the Council of Europe have pointed out over the years in several reports that Estonia should take more positive measures to speed up and simplify the acquisition of citizenship. Hans ten Feld, representative of the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees at the Baltic and Nordic Regional Office, stressed the need to resolve the problem of citizenship quickly in a speech held in the Riigikogu on 17 May 2011. As Estonia has not joined the international conventions for the protection of different stateless people, it believes that politicians should engage in a dialogue to simplify the regulation on citizenship. Mr Feld stressed that Estonia could primarily solve the issue of the citizenship of the children of stateless persons born here, giving them the birth of Estonian citizenship. He stressed that citizenship provides not only a sense of identity but also protection for the state, as well as many civil and political rights. It is not for nothing that it is said about citizenship that it is a right to have rights. There have been major disputes in Estonia on this subject. The most significant of these was the second reading of the draft Citizenship Act in the Riigikogu in 1998, when the current President of the Republic of Estonia and the Estonian Foreign Minister Toomas Hendrik Ilves spoke with an inspiring speech, who stressed that guaranteeing citizenship for children of stateless parents from their birth is the principle of international law that other European countries recognise and observe. In addition, Mr Ilves noted that an argument that should prove that Estonia cannot apply this principle for one reason or another has never been accepted internationally. Nils Muižnieks, Commissioner for Human Rights of the Council of Europe, made a number of recommendations to Estonia in his report last year. One point he pointed out was the need to tackle the reform of citizenship, which would allow children born here to automatically acquire citizenship. He referred to the fact that there were 1181 stateless children under the age of 15 in Estonia as of last year. He found that although the state has created an opportunity for parents to apply for citizenship for children in Estonia, the state is responsible for the emergence of stateless persons. It should be noted that the plan of the Reform Party and the Socialist Coalition states that the return to citizenship should be completed. Coalition partners have found that children whose parents are both stateless and have the right to Estonian citizenship will receive Estonian citizenship if their parents do not give it up. On the other hand, this is not in line with the position of the Council of Europe's Commissioner for Human Rights, Nils Muižnieks, according to which all children born here should be granted Estonian citizenship automatically. The author considers that one of the main reasons why we must allow these children to become citizens is that it is necessary to end the endless transmission of problems from recent history and the burden on parents and the state with endless bureaucratic procedures. Citizenship guarantees better human rights for children born here. This is confirmed by the Commissioner for Human Rights in his report. Now, the objectives of the bill. The main objective of the draft is to facilitate the granting of Estonian citizenship to children born in Estonia whose parent is at least one stateless person. The condition is that the parents have lived legally in Estonia for at least five years. It is worth stressing that this is not about giving citizenship to these children, but about naturalisation for children born in Estonia. Therefore, this principle does not conflict with the principle of uniform citizenship in ius sanguine applied in Estonia. It can be said that long debates have resulted in a solution for children whose parents are both stateless, i.e. with a grey passport. At the same time, there is an unresolved problem that arises when the child is born in Estonia, but one of his parents living here has the citizenship of a third country and the other has the so-called grey passport. I'll give you an example. One parent of the child is stateless and the other is a citizen of the Russian Federation. In this case, the parent has the right to apply for Estonian citizenship for his or her child on a simplified basis when the child has reached the age of five. But there's a problem. In order to apply for Estonian citizenship, the child must abandon the Russian Federation. At the same time, the Russian Federation only accepts a declaration to renounce citizenship when the child is 18 years old. Thus, a situation has been created where the right to citizenship is possible, but it is not practically possible to exercise that right. Our proposal will solve this problem. On the other hand, I would stress that the Estonian state invests large sums of money in every young person -- kindergarten, school, vocational school, university, etc. -- but in the current situation, the state does not bring in its own citizens, but in other countries. According to various sources, almost 250 children are born each year in this situation. We believe that this problem needs to be resolved. The Centre Party will propose its own solutions within the framework of the draft. It should be in Estonia's interest to build a strong sense of citizenship in children born here and to give a clear signal to society that children have been treated equally in Estonia and that the future of children is not dependent on the present or past of their parents. In today's open society, it is high time to overcome confrontation on the ground of national relations and take a major step forward in integration. The citizenship acquired at birth would certainly also facilitate the integration of parents, their interests and future ties with the Republic of Estonia. In addition to the above, the draft proposes that applicants over 65 years of age should be exempted from the obligation to pass the Estonian language proficiency examination, as provided for in the Aliens Act for applicants for long-term residence permits for residents over 65 years of age. The coalition plan of the Reform Party and the Social Democratic Party states that it supports the principle that applicants for citizenship over 65 years of age must pass an oral examination. The author considers that people over the age of 65 should be allowed to have a dignified old age and ensure a reasonable attitude of the state towards them, not to create exceptions that allow the problems to continue. The draft also creates the possibility to pass an examination of the knowledge of the Constitution and Citizenship Act of the Republic of Estonia in English or Russian. The aim of the amendment is to promote the integration of older people into Estonian society. They are not exempt from the knowledge of the Estonian Constitution and the Citizenship Act, but they are allowed to pass the exam in a language suitable for them. We are of the opinion that knowledge of the basis of the functioning of the country of residence and its beliefs is also necessary for people who do not speak the Estonian language. According to preliminary data from the 2011 census, there were 383062 people whose native language is Russian. We need to understand the difficulties faced by older people over 65 in adapting to another language. At the same time, the Russian language has been recognised as the language of a minority in Estonia, i.e. a foreign language that has been used as a native language by persons of a minority in Estonia. English is the world's most common language of business and communication. In the interests of Estonia, it is reasonable that stateless persons apply for Estonian citizenship rather than for any other country. This will help to ensure the integrity of the country and will result in an increase in internal security and people's security, in addition to everything else. Justice and justice exist in society. In a democratic country, they harmonize. Unfortunately, there is no harmony created by the Citizenship Act in Estonia. For example, a person who speaks a language at level B1 must be able to explain the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia and the Citizenship Act. Anyone who knows the requirements of this language level will understand that it would actually require at least a B2 level. I also want to refer to justice. This is particularly the case for people over 65 years of age. These people have proven to be decent while living in Estonia... I wanted to say citizens, but I have to say they're residents. They have contributed with their lives to the independence and development of the Estonian state. They have given birth to Estonian children and raised them here. They have paid taxes properly and thus supported the Estonian state. I think that now is the time for the Estonian state to respect and trust these people. In addition to everything else, it should be acknowledged that during difficult times of crisis they had to cope with their own lives, not only economically but also morally, they had to find the strength to live on. It is no secret that the situation in Ida-Virumaa is the most acute, wages are among the lowest in Estonia, unemployment is the highest in Estonia, but prices are the same as elsewhere. I also stress that the resources of Estonian language teachers in Ida-Virumaa are limited. This was also acknowledged by former Minister of Education and Science Tõnis Lukas. We acknowledge these people, they are already potential good citizens of the Republic of Estonia. Finally, I repeat that in today's open society, it is high time to overcome the opposition to national relations and to take a major step forward in integration. Thank you for your attention.

' That's very right, it coincides with Nils Muizhnieks' view. But then you go on: 'The coalition partners have found that in the case of children whose parents are both stateless and who have the right to Estonian citizenship, Estonian citizenship will be granted if the parents do not give it up. " Nils Muizhnieks speaks of the reproduction of citizenshiplessness, i.e. when parents do not have citizenship. You're talking about giving citizenship automatically. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U205	The contradiction was, let's just say that the law and the coalition didn't solve this issue, because there's nothing you can do automatically here, but the position was that automatically allows citizenship to be granted at birth. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U216	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen, This draft law was discussed in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs on 9 June. The Commission's debate was different from today's debate in that the draft was presented by Yana Toom in the Commission, not Eldar Efendiev. It is also worth noting that Siiri Leskov, adviser to the Migration and Border Guard Policy Department of the Ministry of Interior, was present at the meeting of the Commission on behalf of the Government of the Republic. The Commission highlighted the main issues that the draft seeks to regulate: the exemption of applicants over 65 years of age from the requirement to know the Estonian language, the possibility of taking an examination of the knowledge of the Constitution and the Citizenship Act in Russian and English, and the partial transition to the ius soli principle, i.e. if one parent has a grey passport and the other is a citizen of a third country, in particular the Russian Federation, there will also be the possibility of obtaining Estonian citizenship. All these aspects raised a number of questions and received criticism. According to the representative of the Government of the Republic, the wording of the draft is erroneous in that it allows all applicants for citizenship to pass an examination of the knowledge of the Constitution and the Citizenship Act in Estonian, Russian or English, not only for the elderly. It was pointed out that if it is appealed that the draft is trying to regulate what is stated in the Coalition Agreement between the Reform Party and the Social Democratic Party, that is not the case. This agreement provides for the exemption of applicants over the age of 65 from the written part of the language examination, but the draft provides for a wider exemption from language testing. The chairman of the committee, Mr Maruse, raised the question of whether there is a country where the examination required to apply for citizenship can be carried out in another language. Siri Leskov's answer was that such a country is not known. Several members of the committee stressed that the principle of ius soli does not coincide in any way with the principle of ius sanguinis in Estonia. Yes, there may be a problem that more citizens of the Russian Federation come from among children, but this change here, as came out in the round of questions and answers, is specific in that we can give Estonian citizenship, but we cannot in any way free these young people from the citizenship of another country. In such a case, there would be rather uncomfortable disputes, at least as long as there is a ban on double citizenship in Estonia. Several members of the Commission pointed out that the drafters, at least in the Commission, tried to serve the matter in such a way that when the simplified procedure was written into the law for applicants for citizenship born in 1930 and earlier, they said it was a mistake, in fact people over the age of 65 were actually thinking. This claim was criticised for the fact that those people who are now over 65 years of age were much younger and more shaky when the rule was introduced, and they probably would have had the opportunity to take the exams if they had been willing to do so. This part of the arguments put forward by the drafters concerning the claims made by the Council of Europe's Human Rights Commissioner, Nils Muižnieks, is also somewhat misleading. He has wanted citizenship primarily for those children who would otherwise remain stateless, not for those who are entitled to the citizenship of a third country. The debate resulted in a vote in the Commission. It was first proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 17 June. It was consensually satisfied. 17 June was now two days ago, but we are still living under the star of this agenda. It was also proposed to reject the draft at first reading, supported by 6 members of the Commission, 2 against and no abstentions. The speaker was appointed rapporteur for the Commission. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U281	Let's move on to item 20. The Chancellor of Justice's proposal to align § 5 (2) of the Abortion and Sterilization Act with the Constitution (No 27) begins the debate. Indrek Tederi, Chancellor of Justice of the Speaking Chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U282	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Riigikogu! I turned to the Riigikogu some time ago with the proposal to bring the provision into line with the Constitution, which I consider to be unduly restricting the minor's right to decide on the termination of his or her pregnancy. I will then explain the starting points and the reasons for my proposal. Paragraph 5 (2) of the Abortion and Sterilization Act prohibits the termination of a minor's pregnancy without the consent of his or her legal representative or, if the representative disagrees, without the permission of the court. Thus, a representative's consent can be denied with the permission of the court, but in any case the legal representative must be informed of the pregnancy of the parent or guardian, regardless of the circumstances that would justify not informing the parents, for example when there is a risk of mental or physical violence against the child by the legal representative. Therefore, the contested provision does not allow for the degree of maturity of the minor to be taken into account or to consider whether it would be in the interest of the minor to include a legal representative. Such a rigid and non-negotiable rule is contrary to the Constitution. I will explain why. Regardless of minority, man has fundamental rights. This means that a minor also has a general right of liberty and a fundamental right of personality under § 19 of the Constitution. This fundamental right includes a person's right to self-determination, including in matters concerning the continuation of gender and the creation of a family. This fundamental right also gives rise to the right of everyone to freely decide whether to intervene physically. § 19 of the Constitution is therefore also relevant in the field of health care, i.e. it must be taken into account when reviewing a person, operating, administering medicinal products. This provision of the Constitution also applies to the relationship between a minor and his or her parents. True, parents have the right and responsibility to care for their children. However, the constitutional custody of parents is limited to the rights and interests of the child. The same is true of the Convention on the Rights of the Child, which is binding and directly applicable to the Republic of Estonia. In this way, the minor's freedom of decision must be recognised in accordance with the development of his or her abilities, i.e. in view of the maturity of the minor. Parental power is reduced to the degree of maturity of the child. The Convention on Human Rights and Biomedicine, which is binding on Estonia, contains a requirement that a medical professional may provide health services when the person has voluntarily given his or her informed consent. In other words, the patient usually decides whether or not to allow medical intervention after considering all the pros and cons. The Convention states that the role of a minor in the provision of medical care increases in proportion to his or her age and degree of maturity. The Convention on Human Rights and Biomedical thus emphasises the decision-making power of a minor according to the development of his or her abilities, or maturity. The privacy of the minor must be guaranteed, together with the decision-making power. The right to privacy derives from § 26 of the Constitution. This right is a right of every person, which includes information about the condition of a person's body and spirit. The privacy of fundamental rights also applies to the relationship between a doctor and a patient. The doctor must ensure confidentiality so that the person has the courage to contact the doctor for help at all. This means that if the minor is provided with health care with the agreement he/she has informed, the patient's trust is also protected against his/her parent. A parent does not always have the right to receive information from a doctor about the provision of health care to a child. This is also true in the event of the termination of a minor's pregnancy. Thus, when it comes to the issue of minor abortion, parental custody must recede before the right of a pregnant woman to self-determination. In so doing, a minor cannot be deprived of his or her discretion in matters relating to his or her health and physical integrity solely because he or she is under 18 years of age. Therefore, where a minor is able to give informed consent to the termination of his or her pregnancy, it is not justified to require the consent of the parent or other party. A teenager also has the right to privacy and to the protection of a doctor's and patient's trust in a situation where he or she reasonably opposes the involvement of a legal representative. My contested provision precludes the independent decision-making of a person under the age of 18 in favour of his parents or guardian. In doing so, a woman's doctor must not grant a reasoned exception to a teenager who is capable of considering the pros and objections in a responsible manner. The doctor must also require the parents or guardian to be informed if this would be contrary to the interests of the pregnant woman for reasons attributable to them. By way of derogation, it is possible to replace the consent of a legal representative with the permission of a court, which is of no use, since the precondition for obtaining a court's authorisation is to inform the legal representative, who must have refused to give consent. As a result of the analysis, I find that § 5 (2) of the Abortion and Sterilization Act is too rigid and therefore contrary to the Constitution. This provision prohibits the termination of a minor's pregnancy without the consent of his or her legal representative or the permission of the court. Thus, the provision restricts the right to self-determination below 18 in a disproportionate manner by making it overly dependent on its parents or guardians. Nor does this provision guarantee a minor's right to privacy and health protection. I'll explain. Under 18 years of age, this provision does not allow the pregnancy to be interrupted safely and without dedicating parents or guardians. In so doing, the provision does not allow consideration to be given to the degree of maturity of the minor and does not allow consideration to be given to whether the involvement of a legal representative is in the interest of the minor. In my opinion, when bringing this anti-constitutional provision into line with the Constitution, the legislator could consider whether to restore the solution previously in force, so that the issue of abortion of a minor is governed by the provisions of the Law of Obligations Act governing the provision of health care services to a minor. The Law of Obligations Act contains, in my opinion, sufficient balanced regulation on this issue. According to this law, for patients with reduced capacity, the right to health care is subject to the principle of informed consent, in so far as the patient is able to consider the pros and cons in a responsible manner. Otherwise, his legal representative will decide for him. If the decision of the legal representative is likely to harm the patient's interests, the healthcare provider may not comply with it. If this provision is good enough for a minor to decide on his or her own cancer treatment or kidney surgery, why can he or she not decide to discontinue his or her pregnancy under the same provision? Before the termination of a pregnancy, a minor may be offered psychological and crisis counselling in addition to other usual counselling. Thank you!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U283	Thank you, Chancellor of Justice. You have at least one question. Terje Trei, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U290	Thank you, Chancellor of Justice. No further questions. Rein Lang, member of the Constitutional Committee of the Riigikogu, would like to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U291	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U293	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U296	Thank you for your question. I think that, in the case of minors, abortion could be regulated as was the case until 2009, i.e. the Law of Obligations Act. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U301	Honourable waitress! Indeed, also in the Committee on Social Affairs, when we were together years ago, these issues were being discussed. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U302	Do you mean nobody tells him to have an abortion if he doesn't want to? Is that what you thought? I didn't understand.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U303	Good waitress, thank you! No further questions. Is there a desire to negotiate? It's what you want. I'm opening negotiations. Speaking chair of the Riigikogu Kaia Iva, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U304	Honourable listeners! In the general principle, of course, we must agree with the Chancellor of Justice that the absolute prohibition on the termination of a minor's pregnancy without the consent of a legal representative, usually the consent of the parents or the permission of the court, unduly restricts the definition of a young woman, a teenager. This is particularly true in cases where family circumstances are complicated, relationships with parents are critical or violent, when a minor is without parental care or, for example, an extreme situation, rape, and the resulting pregnancy. It is particularly perilous that an elder has the right to demand that a minor undergo an abortion. This puts a pregnant youth in a particularly restrictive position where a positive decision on unborn life is not up to him. It is therefore necessary to provide for more flexible regulation in which to decide whether to terminate a minor's pregnancy. At the same time, it should be noted that a situation in which a minor does not want or cannot talk to his or her legal representative about matters of such importance, asking for advice or consent is in itself problematic and needs to be dealt with separately. This requires professional advice, analysis of the situation or supervision of a social adviser, child protection or other specialist. It is important to note that making regulation more flexible and increasing the right of a minor to self-determination cannot be a step to the other extreme. A minor cannot remain alone on these issues; all responsibility cannot be on the shoulders of a young person in this highly complex situation. Of course, in addition to his or her own opinion, the most important thing is the attitude, support, and opinion of the child's father. That is what we have not paid attention to right now. The possibility of a couple of joint decisions and responsibilities could also be one of the main objectives. However, the role of a minor parent cannot become insignificant. At the same time, we must not overlook the fact that, on a number of other issues, we are greatly restricting the right of the minor to self-determination, which Terje drew to the attention here, for example, the consent of a legal representative is required when he takes up his duties. One might ask whether it is more difficult for a minor to decide whether to do maintenance work in the park during the summer vacation and whether it is more important for a young person's right to self-determination than to decide on the life of an unborn child. Therefore, we agree with the Chancellor of Justice that the restrictions must be changed, but the regulation that is being introduced must be considered on all sides and the rights of the young person must be equally supported and adequately consulted. Adequate, evidence-based and enforceable counselling supports women's choices and contributes to coping with changes during pregnancy, preparing for childbirth, breast-feeding, and adapting to their mother's role. Its attachment to the applicable, restrictive regulation is necessary and unavoidable. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U305	Thank you. No more calls, I'll close the negotiations. Let us begin the preparation of the final vote. Kaia Iva, please! You were in a hurry, you were.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U306	Thank you. I want a ten-minute break.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U310	Let's move on to item 21. The second reading of Act 692 on the Implementation of the General Part of the Environmental Code Act initiated by the Government of the Republic begins. Please present Tõnis Kõiv, Member of the Environment Committee of the Riigikogu Chair!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U311	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Draft Act 612 amending the General Part of the Environmental Code Act and related amendments to other laws was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 10 March 2014. The first reading of this draft was completed on 9 April and the deadline for submitting amendments was set at 5.15 p.m. on 23 April 2014. There were no proposals for the draft. Draft Act implementing the General Part of the Environmental Code Act 668 was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 12 May. Its first reading was completed on 21 May and the deadline for submitting amendments was set at 12 noon on 30 May. One amendment was tabled by the speaker on draft 668. At its meeting on 9 June, the Committee on the Environment decided in principle to merge drafts 612 and 668. The new bill's number is 692. The initiator of the drafts also agreed to merge the drafts. The Committee on the Environment prepared a second reading of the draft at its meeting of 9th, 10th and 16th June, attended by representatives of the Ministry of the Environment. Hannes Veinla, a lecturer on environmental law, took part in the meeting on 9 June and gave the members of the Commission an overview of the General Part of the Environmental Code Act. The amendments tabled were discussed at the meeting on 10 and 16 June. You have received a list of amendments which I am not going to read out. However, I would like to draw attention to a letter received by the Committee on the Environment from Kodala OÜ. In his letter, he asked us to put forward an amendment on the extension of the construction ban zone. Since there is no reason for this amendment from the standpoint of the Ministry of the Environment, there is no contradiction between the provisions of the law and § 40 of the current Nature Conservation Act already allows the construction ban zone to be extended and narrowed, there is a regulation to that end, the Commission decided not to propose an amendment to this effect. At its meeting on 9 June, the Committee on the Environment decided to send draft law 612 amending the General Part of the Environmental Code Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws, and draft law 668 implementing the General Part of the Environmental Code Act to the second reading of the new text entitled 'Proposal for the Implementation of the General Part of the Environmental Code Act' (692 SE) on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 18 June 2014 and to propose that the second reading be stopped. When the second reading of the draft is completed, we have a proposal to put the draft to the final vote. All these decisions were taken by the Committee on the Environment in a consensual manner. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U314	Thank you, good questioner! When we look at § 14 of Bill 692, or § 14 of the amendment to the bill, I see here an amendment which provides for an amendment to the text of § 26 which regulates the reimbursement of costs for causing environmental damage or the risk of such damage. However, if not to look at a specific section but to answer the question in substance, it must be said that the different dates of entry into force depend on the stage of preparation for the implementation of the law and when the entry into force of those parts is possible. All these deadlines are based on the principle: as quickly as possible, but not as quickly as possible.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U315	Peter Laurson, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U316	Thank you, Mr President. Good waitress! According to the draft law, § 35 (3) of the Act reads as follows: "In the event of a tenting and other permanent stay in an unprepared and unmarked place, the noticeable visibility and hearing distance of the dwelling must be avoided. Open terrain must be avoided at least 150 metres from the home. " What is the reason why you have to stay 150 metres away from the house? What harm can a closer camping do to this house?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U317	Thank you, good questioner! This is one of the necessary and important changes to this bill, which protects people living in beautiful places from excessive disturbance. There are two criteria. One is a minimum distance of 150 metres; the other is that it is necessary to avoid visibility. The tent itself has no negative effect on anyone, but the environmental disturbance resulting from the camping process, which may mean, for example, a variety of voices and noises, can disturb people. Such noise should be avoided during camping and other activities. 150 metres is the subjective distance determined in metres, it is assumed to be sufficient. In fact, it may appear that this is not enough, in which case campers simply have to submit to prayer to stop disturbing them.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U437	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, According to § 143 of the Constitution, the Chancellor of Justice gives an annual overview of how the Riigikogu and the Government have complied with the Constitution. So do I now. Recently, it has become common practice in our society to criticise Parliament's activities and work. The criticism forgets the complexity of the legislator's work and the frequent recognition of its positive results. It is difficult to decide the weighty issues that shape Estonia's life every day, and it is inevitable that decisions must also be made from time to time, which may not be pleasant for all groups of society, but are necessary for some big goals. Thank you for being on the right track and taking a lot of weighty decisions for society. In recent times, I particularly recognise the legislative amendment adopted in June, which facilitates the application for citizenship for young people who, despite their lack of a residence permit, have been living in Estonia on a permanent basis, in a simple way, young people without a residence permit will be able to acquire citizenship more easily. This is not just a minor improvement, but a really important step, I think. I am of the opinion that this is humane, but it also contributes to strengthening constitutional order. As we know, it is the duty of an Estonian citizen to be loyal to the constitutional order and to defend Estonia's independence. Right and money. At the beginning of my term of office, in 2008, the economic crisis began. When economic development slows down, complex and unpopular decisions need to be taken frequently. In times of crisis, the legislator is much more difficult to be than in times of economic boom. Therefore, both you and your predecessors have had a very difficult time and it will continue. No new economic boom is foreseen to be decided on the basis of the Spring Economic Forecast of the Ministry of Finance. The legislator must therefore continue to make very complex decisions: it must ensure that there is sufficient money in the public purse to finance all social expenditure, while ensuring that those who bring in the money do not get too much blow from the planned changes in the tax and economic environment. It may be noted that the statements received relate either directly or indirectly to money. I note that social expenditure in Estonia is significant. I will use the National Audit Office's overview of the use and disposal of state assets 2012. In 2006: 'The largest share of fixed expenditure is social expenditure, i.e. expenditure on pensions, health insurance, family allowances, etc., which is already almost half of the state budget. However, the rapid increase in social expenditure and the high proportion of the state budget have not led to a significant increase in pensions or to improved access to health care. 'The growth in social expenditure for the coming year will be significantly increased by the planned increases in child benefits. The Chancellor of Justice is not an entrepreneur; he does not produce money. However, justice must also have a material covering. Paying attention to the welfare of children is a right and noble idea. However, when dealing with one problem, we must not forget the others. Perhaps every promise must have a financial cover and must also take into account the people who are helping to fill the Treasury. If the business environment is unduly deteriorating, we will not have those who are helping to make money for the social budget in the long term. The legislator must therefore be a true balancer. It is very important for businesses to have a sound legal order. There can be no credibility without the possibility of relying on a commitment made unambiguously by the State. Let us begin by maintaining the legislator's ability to make political choices. The rapidly evolving events of recent years have shown, in particular, that the legislator and the government sometimes need to change previous decisions in order to adapt to the situation. According to the Constitution, this is not impossible, so the principle of legitimate expectations in critical situations must be interpreted narrowly. The protected area of this principle is not all changes in the legal order that may affect a person. In some cases, however, a law or regulation is adopted which cannot be interpreted in any way other than an unambiguous promise. This was the case, for example, for environmental charges, so-called mining fees. When such a clear term commitment is suddenly changed in a harmful direction for businesses, their resentment is understandable. In the event of an unambiguous breach of the promise, it seriously undermines the credibility of the legal order and increases the uncertainty of businesses, which in turn hampers economic development. So there is no rational or constitutional action. In that case, the Chancellor of Justice must also act in such a way as to show that the Constitution works. Besides defending the rule of law, it gives people faith and confidence and allows people to reduce bitterness because power does what it wants. It strengthens the foundation of society. In this case, we will show that the Constitution works even if there are large sums and complex choices involved. To conclude this part, I stress that the desire to obtain budget money quickly cannot go beyond the need to maintain the credibility of state authority, all the more so since, in the long term, it is the credibility of the state that guarantees a sound business environment and, therefore, the growth of budgetary money. In this sense, declaring a regulation unconstitutional in the Supreme Court cannot in any way be regarded as a loss of government. Rather, it is an important step in the area of constitutional restrictions, which guarantees not only the business environment, but more generally the rule of law of Estonia as a whole. With regard to the reliability of the rules, it is also important that the legislator itself treats judgments with respect and seriousness. I would like to repeat what Priit Pikamäe, President of the Supreme Court, said: 'It is very important that the legislator and executive power also comply with the judgments. " As Mr Pikamäe pointed out, the legislator is very weak in enforcing judgments. There's a case I took to court that scratches me. There is still no new set of rules on the remuneration for the handling of technical networks and installations. I consider it unacceptable that the legislator has left the landowners and businesses here in the mess of disputes between themselves. The consequence is that 10000 forest owners will not receive any appropriate compensation for damaged forest land. This failure by the legislator increases distrust of the legislator and the legal order. But at the same time we love the law, we're a judicial nation. Now I'm talking about money more widely, but shorter. Over the years, there has been a lot of talk in Parliament about money when discussing the annual budget. In essence, the State Budget Act is one of the most important laws. Our country's budget is small compared to the budgets of the large countries, just about eight billion. We can argue endlessly, but it is clear that we want a fair and fair distribution of money. It is inevitable that sharing money will create tensions and emotions. But let us look to other European countries. The question may arise as to whether money is more important than the Constitution, the principles of justice. President Toomas Hendrik Ilves stated at the Lennart Meri conference that Europe tends to make political decisions on financial grounds: 'It is not ideology, it is money. There's not enough talk about the soft power of money. It's an elephant in a room we don't want to admit. " That is what Ilves said about the West's sanctions against Russia's aggression. He added that, strangely enough, the countries that are not really economically dependent on Russia have taken a lukewarm view of strong economic sanctions and referred to the problem in the UK that there is no suggestion of sacrificing anything in the London business sector. Does the Republic of Estonia speak only of money and the right is retreating? Do we have the power of justice, or is money the only thing that dominates? I hope and am convinced that, of course, I do not want to close my eyes either, that the right must pay. I hope that the right provision of the Constitution is not a facade. The right must not be deformed. But it's been done in history. Unfortunately, money and physical power are dominating. We are not naives and we have consensually used public money to strengthen security. So we can look to the future calmly and joyfully. The world isn't just about money. Constitution, morality, and faith are important. The latter, too, have full constitutional implications. As an example, I will mention the case when the publisher questioned whether it was constitutional to require conscripts to make a vow. In the past, the Chancellor of Justice had taken the view that, under the Defence Forces Service Act in force until 31 March 2013, it was not mandatory for conscripts to make a vow. However, after an in-depth analysis and procedure, I came to the conclusion that under the Defence Forces Service Act, which entered into force on 1 April 2013, a conscript was required to make a vow and this obligation was in accordance with the Constitution. At the same time, the oath is much wider and more serious: according to § 54 of the Constitution, Estonia's independence is protected. Finally. Estonia is an everlasting and democratic state. Foreigners ruled in Estonia for about 700 years, but their footprints have now disappeared from here and, for example, the Toompea Knight's Building is ours, the Parliament's. Thank you!

The Chancellor of Justice has said: 'The withdrawal of the draft is a matter of fundamental importance which must be decided by the Government of the Republic as a collegial body. Due to the meaning of § 87 (4) of the Constitution, § 103 (1) (4) of the Constitution and § 90 (1) (4) of the Riigikogu Rules of Procedure Act, the withdrawal of the draft submitted by the Government of the Republic to the Riigikogu cannot be decided by the Minister alone and the government cannot delegate such decision-making powers to the Minister. Any withdrawal of the draft must be preceded by a collegial decision taken by the Government of the Republic in this regard.
