Let me put it this way: it is not only up to our decision to deploy defence forces on missions that we make proposals and offers, but we also look at what we can do from the point of view of using human resources and what we think is reasonable. We certainly do not intend to deploy more members of the Defence Forces to this operation this year. The fear that the number of six is too small is not justified at this time. If there should be a need for more members of the Defence Forces in the future, then, as has been said, the draft extension of the deadline for the various missions will come to this Chamber already during this year and it will certainly be possible to revisit these limits as well. At the moment, the situation was not sufficiently flexible as the maximum number of members of the Defence Forces seconded to the mission overlapd with the number of people on the mission. Hence the need to come before the Riigikogu earlier to change the draft mission.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U620	Thank you. Please, Margus Tsahkna, second question!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U621	Thank you. Minister, thank you very much. I'm asking, from the background, what's going on in this area. In fact, Iraq has broken down now, not de jure, but central control of the territory has disappeared. Syria is his neighbour. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U622	Thank you very much. Please, Sven Sester, second question!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U623	Thank you, Mr President. Good minister! Since we are authorising up to six members of the Defence Forces instead of three members of the Defence Forces in this draft, I would like to ask. How do such agreements take place with partners? Are there any "limits" between each country to determine how many soldiers he sends on a mission? Is it dependent on something? So, what were we doing on six? Perhaps in a few words you will give members of the Riigikogu an overview of this.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U624	Thank you. For each mission, the 'arms' or mission leaders will establish the needs of the mission and draw up a list of capabilities needed to achieve the mission's objectives, and then states will declare their willingness to display these capabilities. Sometimes it's easy to make troops, and there's more to offer than they ask. Unfortunately, in many cases, the opposite is true, the need is greater than the willingness of countries to contribute. Certainly different missions also mean that many of the world's countries, not even the most prosperous, are more willing to contribute to UN missions because they are financed differently. NATO-led missions are governed by a principle, which is described in English as costes lie where they fall (each one bears its own costs). True, here too, in some cases, another ally is willing to compensate for certain costs. But usually, the military forces work to specify the needs of the mission in terms of different capabilities, and then the states themselves declare their contributions and the Head of Mission, the Steering Organisation or the State will say which of these capabilities they offer will actually need and be ready to use.

The tensions in the region are not only added to the Syrian civil war, but, as the previous issues have been pointed out, so is the situation in Iraq. The purpose of this mission, as has been said, is to monitor the fulfilment of the armistice conditions. Of course, a situation in which all countries in the region would have peace agreements and diplomatic relations between themselves and, by means of so-called normal political channels, would create prerequisites for lasting peace. But this is a broader issue, the discussion of which goes beyond the framework of the draft.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U635	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The domestic policy and domestic policy interests of a small country must be subordinated to foreign and security policy interests. For a small country to be able to successfully realise its foreign and security policy interests in the world, it is important that there is consensus. The general practice has been that security-political parliamentary mandates have been given by the Riigikogu and decisions on this subject have been taken in a spirit of consensus and benevolently. The Fatherland and Res Publica Union, being a party to the opposition, is still supporting the government in the process of dealing with this draft decision, based on this spirit. If you look at the practice of issuing mandates in the freed-up Estonian political history, it can be said that the spirit of consensus has been almost without precedent. It is true that the controversy about the Iraqi mission, which was around the world, the parliaments of the various European countries and the United States Parliament, also reached this Chamber, which took place in the final phase of the mission. A precedent arose when the deployment of Estonian Defence Forces in the Central African Republic was discussed as part of the European Union mission. It was a negative precedent, and it also collected votes against in the hall. It was under the leadership of Marianne Mikko that very serious criticism was made. I am pleased that the Minister of Defence, Sven Mikser, did not allow himself to be shaken by his desire to engage in daily political affairs in the Estonian defence policy, but gave his mandate to enforce the decision of the Riigikogu. This particular mission is two dimensions. One relates to Estonia's foreign policy initiative, the request to stand out more positively in the UN, the long-term goal of becoming a member of the UN Security Council as a temporary member. The active contribution to the missions is certainly one of the elements of this plan for the collection of support. The second dimension stems from the principle of collective defence, which provides not only that the allies are prepared to contribute to the security of the Estonian state, but also that Estonia is a country that contributes significantly to the international collective defence system according to its capabilities. Believe me, these resources are the expenses, equipment and armaments, this political initiative, but also the willingness to deploy Estonian Defence Forces, will also give real results in the form that our state is protected. The draft which is currently under way to increase the membership of the UN mission is certainly included in this objective, so it is appropriate for Parliament to support it and adopt a positive position. Speaking about the mission policy more broadly, Estonia's IRL believes that the missions must have an active state. Secondly, it is extremely important that Estonia contribute to the most important missions for both the European Union and the UN and NATO missions. This will increase our visibility and strengthen our partnerships. Above all, in the case of major, multi-state missions, cooperation with our major military allies, with the United States, with the United Kingdom, with France, and with other countries in the Western space, with greater military strength, is certainly extremely important. The next aspect of the mission policy is that, in the major missions of both the European Union and NATO, we must not only accept that we put out a flag in the form of one or two officers of staff, but we must be prepared to contribute to strengthening the element of military cooperation with actual military capabilities based on the physical size of our country. Looking at the European Union's major missions, as well as NATO's most important missions, it can be said that Estonia has pursued this policy. We've been an active country, we've contributed to the actual military capabilities. It has given us invaluable security and foreign policy capital, both in good allied relations and undoubtedly in the fact that our defence forces have gained practical experience. Decisions on missions are, on the one hand, important for the security policy of the country, foreign policy interests, but, on the other hand, there are always important people, defence forces, in making such decisions. The Riigikogu makes decisions about these choices when contributing to different missions. It is certainly always appropriate for all mission decisions to assess the risks that may arise from missions. The support of all Estonian society and the Estonian state to our mission soldiers during pre-mission preparation is extremely important. Their families must also be supported in the pre-mission phase. During the mission, it must be ensured that the Estonian Defence Forces have the best equipment available and that military measures are taken. It is certainly important to support the members of the Defence Forces and their family members after the mission. I am pleased that we were able to adopt this spring in Parliament a package of laws on defence vegetarians, which, when it comes into force in August, will give real support to these men and women. The opinion of the IRL is that there must be consensus on the mission policy. In the spirit of this consensus, we support this draft. Secondly, we consider it important that missions are guided by the longer-term security interests that I referred to earlier. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U681	Good chairman of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen, The bill, which created a very passionate debate, is an epoch-creative for two reasons. There have been individual attempts to regulate maritime affairs, but then the coalition parties have said that at present there is no need to make any concessions to maritime affairs, because this is a natural part of the economy. At the same time, the most important aspect of the creation of the epoch is that this bill has been initiated by a group of members of the Riigikogu, united by a single understanding. These are members of the union of transit and logistics of the Riigikogu. It's a very active group. At our recent meetings, we have been very aware of the importance of maritime affairs, railways and also ports and their impact on the Estonian economy. As a result, I am extremely pleased that for the first time in the very long time that I have been in the Riigikogu, a draft has been brought here, which has been initiated by delegates across political parties, and there are quite a number of opposition members among them, but this draft has not been put in a drawer. This kind of cultural change is what makes me claim that this bill is an epoch-creative one. Let's think, even Arto Aas, Remo Holsmer and Kalev Lillo have signed here, who we could consider to be the people who will never stand up to the ruling Prime Minister and government of our party. What is important is that this draft will enable Parliament, for the first time in a long time, to show that MEPs are the highest authority and that the government must carry out the tasks that MEPs propose to the government. For this to be the case in the future, I call on Members of Parliament to set up support groups and associations, more and more. Then perhaps the questions that we will get out of it will disappear, whether there is a sense of corruption here, and whether anyone should immediately go to jail after something very substantive is done in a positive way. It's not like that. I call for this draft to be supported because it is a very strongly restorative draft of the actual content and status of Parliament, but at the same time it contributes greatly to the development of Estonian maritime affairs. This is a fair bill that does not significantly damage or affect the state budget. If I break a gun and I need someone to protect the country from, I'll take the other gun. That is exactly the same principle in this draft. If one ship broke down, I'll take another ship, but it can't be followed by punishment, especially financial. These dates are as follows: 1 January 2003, 1 January 2004, 8 January 2004, 25 April 2004, 5 June 2004, 3 June 2005, 1 July 2005, 18 November 2005, 1 June 2006, 16 November 2008, 1 July 2009, 10 July 2009, 1 January 2010, 20 October 2009, 1 January 2011, 1 January 2011 (with the indication that the amendment will enter into force on the day fixed by the Council of the European Union in its Decision repealing the derogation for the Republic of Estonia under Article 140 (2) of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union pursuant to the Decision of the Council of the European Union of 13 July 2010), 1 October 2010, 1 January 2011 (later this date of entry into force was amended and the new date of entry into force will be 1 July 2011), 2 January 2011 (partially this amendment entered into force on 1 January 2012), 18 March 2011, 1 January 2014 (the new date of entry into force was amended, 1 July 2014), 14 July 2011, 1 January 2012, 1 January 2012 (i.e. a different date of entry into force), 1 September 2012, 1 July 2013, 1 July 2013 (partly 1 January 2014), 1 July 2013, 9 June 2013 (partly 1 January 2014), 1 January 2014), 1 January 2014 (the entry into force date will be partially amended, new), and the last of 1 July 2014), and the date of 1 July 2014. The point of my speech is that, if we look not only at this vital law, but also at all other laws, in terms of the quality of the law, the aim should undoubtedly be to achieve a situation in which, in matters that are very important and require investment, but also in matters relating to the life, health and safety of people, the law would be of a higher quality in that sense, so that the dates of entry into force of the various Acts would not be so close. We may think that one vessel owner has a thousand other concerns than the date on which, in part, in full or depending on a decision of the European Union, one or another change will enter into force. Some points also apply to boats which are simply used to spend leisure time, with the length of the boat being 2,5 metres. These are boats whose owners may not even be aware of this law, not to mention that they will be able to comply with the constant changes in the Maritime Safety Act. What can be done, for example, with § 16, which literally says, in itself, very nicely that the ship is fit for navigation when it is built in accordance with good sea practice? I believe that this knowledge has existed in practice since the first ships were built. Of course, maritime practice has become better and better in history. However, the question in itself is whether such principles of good practice and good will are justified and worth repeating in law. If a liner had been paid for a waterway charge and the ship went to repair, the ship that replaced the same route, which was just as large, had to pay that fee. That was unfair. Why couldn't this irrational situation be resolved in a direct discussion with the Maritime Administration? That's a strange question. The Maritime Administration could reasonably have said, in the same definition of a waterway charge, that this fee could not be charged twice. Two ships didn't go to the port, only one ship did. But what does it mean if the reward is taken twice instead of once? Competitiveness is declining. Those ships that had to pay twice were no longer competitive with those who paid once. Such behaviour may have resulted in some companies simply losing their jobs. The ships aren't being repaired just for fun, the ship has to be under proper technical control. Therefore, a double fee should not be charged. A similar situation, which does not take into account the rates, is emerging on the railways. Yes, we can, of course, talk about whether this is necessary at all, as the Estonian state earns a relatively large income from the tow service, but the Economic Commission could certainly carry out an economic analysis as to whether such a change would increase the flow of goods. At the same time, we have increased one trade flow year after year. This is not only about maritime safety, but also about the safety of human health and the environment. It's oil transport in the Baltic Sea. About 1995 around 25 million tonnes of oil were transported in the Baltic Sea, Estonia's coastal areas. In 2004, 110 million tonnes were already reached per year and the forecast for 2015 is more than 250 million tonnes of petroleum products per year. In the last 15 years, there has been a significant increase in the volume of tankers. Compared to 1995, the risk of oil pollution has increased at least five times. I'm asking for three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U738	Three minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U739	At present, the Estonian state is not ready to take effective assistance measures in the event of a major oil spill. The situation has improved considerably in recent years. New large tankers must have two bottoms. So we've improved our preparedness, but certainly not at enough speed. There have also been cases in recent years when an oil tanker has gone ashore. Fortunately, there has not been a very large oil spill. But the oil tanker is divided into parts and one part has more than 30,000 litres of oil. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U741	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The draft currently under parliamentary procedure is, in itself, sympathetic, as it has been initiated by the representatives of all the political groups. IRL's view of regulatory regulation of the economic environment is certainly such that the economic environment must, first, operate rationally. Overregulation is under no circumstances a solution. Secondly, in an economic environment, the rules of fair play must prevail. This means that these rules must be uniformly applicable and must not create a distorted competitive situation. Thirdly, there must be general legal values: legal expectation, legal clarity, equal treatment. There are no doubt a number of economic areas where the functioning of free competition is not possible, and these are areas where a rational approach must be particularly well implemented in the interests of consumers and taxpayers and in the general public interest. The principle of legal expectation is a value that we should not only address in economic terms, but it is a question of the functioning of the whole legal environment. The reason for this very thorough procedure of the draft proposal, or the incentive from the point of view of the IRL, is precisely that, next in Parliament's proceedings, there is a draft law which, in our view, does not, firstly, comply with the principle of legal expectation and, secondly, it is not fair in the human view either. The problem of treating the different generations of parents differently has been solved jointly by applying the calculation of retirement years equally to all parents. This desire for the pension system to take into account the contribution that parents have made to raising children, so that they do not receive a lower pension because they have been at home because they have been raising children, also entered the law. The amount required for this purpose in the state budget is large: 86 million euros over three years. However, the amount available is not, of course, excessive from the point of view of a specific parent. The principle is precisely whether or not the state will pay the word. This pension supplement, which we jointly decided to pay parents two years ago to level out inequalities between these and the elderly, is EUR 120 per parent with two children per year. The coalition will solve by delaying the entry into force of the law into an indefinite future, referring to three years, its budgetary problems. However, there is no doubt that such a pattern of conduct provides no guarantee, no guarantee that this three-year limit could not be postponed further or that the decision to pay the pension supplement at all would be revoked. Therefore, when we talk about the economy, about human relations, about the functioning of the state, there are a couple of fundamental values in shaping the whole legal environment that we should adhere to together. First, respect for legitimate expectations. Secondly, the non-breaking of the word given by the state. Thirdly, if we introduced this system because it is more honest with pensioners who have raised children, let us not take a step that is actually damaging this value and is unfair. The long processing of the draft Maritime Safety Act and other good and good laws means protest, not because of our confrontation or our tantrum. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U771	Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U772	Good President! Ladies and gentlemen, I, too, would like to thank all those members of the Riigikogu who initiated the draft law on supplementing the maritime safety law that we are discussing today. This draft will make our maritime industry more competitive by eliminating one completely unjustified charge for ships engaged in regular voyages. The intensity of the use of the waterway is one of the fundamental elements of the calculation of the waterway charge laid down in the Maritime Safety Act. The intensity of the use of the waterway must therefore also be taken into account for ships operating on a regular service. If there's a replacement, one ship gets off the line and the other one comes in, traffic intensity doesn't grow. On the condition that the number of ships and thus the intensity of the use of the waterway do not change when replacing vessels serving the regular service, there is no substantive basis for requiring an additional fee for the waterway. It is well founded that there is a clear exclusion from the possibility that, in the event of replacement of a ship engaged in regular voyages, the debtor would have to pay the waterway charge twice or even twice. Even if the bill deprives the state budget of a certain amount of revenue, the competitiveness of the maritime sector as a whole will increase. Reeders will be exempted from unjustified costs and will have greater economic freedom to relocate ships to optimise their network on line according to market demand. Regular ships mainly serve passengers and vehicles and passenger flows on scheduled routes are strongly dependent on seasons and weather, especially for tourist trips. Estonia's maritime competitiveness needs to be strengthened. Maritime affairs is closely linked to transit and logistics, where jobs producing the highest added value of the Estonian economy and offering the highest wage income are located. I would like to argue with my colleague, Mr Stalnukhin, who said in his speech half an hour ago that maritime affairs do not bring in much, and the peculiarity of Estonian national identity is to talk about maritime affairs. According to 2010 data, 4% of all workers in the Estonian economy worked in the maritime economy in the narrowest sense, including 1% in ports, 1% in the tourism industry, 1% in fisheries, and 1% in passenger and freight transport. Although we know that there is a shipbuilding cluster in Estonia, its size does not make up 1% of employment. At the same time, when looking at the maritime economy in a narrower sense, its share of added value, in 2010, 4% of the workers working in the maritime sector generated 5.5% of the added value. Most of it was created in the port economy. The share of maritime activity in Estonia's employment differs from its share in creating value added. This shows that the maritime industry earns higher than average wages. These figures are not insignificant even now, but they could be much higher. Moreover, these figures only concern maritime affairs in the most direct and narrowest sense. In a broader sense, the maritime income provides a much wider group. For example, in the case of freight transport, carriers, chargers, storers, repackers, stevidors, brokers, ship suppliers, logistics are added. The movement of goods requires financial and insurance services, which the Estonian financial sector certainly wants to offer. Passenger shipping companies make a significant contribution to Tallinn's retail trade, taxiing, hoteling, catering, guide services, and I have even heard about the turnover of dental care and beauty services. The ship is the heart of the sea business and the sea business as one of the most important connections of the Estonian economy to the rest of the world is one of the supporting areas of the Estonian economy, the importance of which must not be underestimated. If the maritime industry is doing badly, other industries that rely on links, especially maritime connections, will also fail. In this sense, the role of maritime affairs must not be underestimated in the context of improving the competitiveness of the Estonian economy and stimulating economic growth. I am pleased that the Riigikogu has dealt seriously with the development of maritime affairs in previous years. The Riigikogu has adopted 'Estonia's Maritime Policy 2012 The aim of the maritime policy was to develop guidelines on maritime sectors in synergy in order to maximise Estonia's potential for the use and preservation of marine resources. The activities provided for in maritime policy shall aim to contribute to the development of the maritime sector, in particular by supporting the development of maritime business, improving the safety of navigation and other maritime activities and protecting the marine and coastal environment and marine cultural heritage. The objectives and activities set out in maritime policy are based on the view that maritime issues and their solutions are closely interlinked and need to be addressed in a coordinated manner. As maritime areas are divided between different ministries, they need to be dealt with in the Single Development Document in order to tackle problems effectively and to support the development of the sector. This approach is also based on the European Commission's Communication 'An Integrated Maritime Policy for the European Union', which calls on Member States to draw up integrated national maritime policy policies. The maritime policy document also gives us the vision of the maritime sector. It is worded as follows: "The Estonian maritime sector is a high-value-added, attractive and sustainable economic sector that ensures the preservation of the marine environment and contributes to the development of the coastal environment and lifestyle. ' Estonia's maritime policy also has a separate chapter on the draft law that we are discussing today. It's international passenger transport. In this area, the aim is to increase the number of passengers on international shipping routes. A very large proportion of foreign tourists arriving in Estonia come here by sea. Although we are expected to reach a saturation point in the number of these tourists, it is still possible to increase it. In the long term, it is necessary to ensure that the objective achieved remains stable. Maritime policy concerns the competitiveness of passenger shipping in this area. There is a need to ensure that our companies are competitive and motivated to increase the number of passengers. In this direction, we are taking one small step today when we are dealing with this bill, especially if we adopt it as a law. Although the maritime policy document does not plan direct subsidies for passenger transport as the most important activities, the task is to constantly monitor the situation in the passenger shipping market, and if it appears that measures supporting passenger shipping in other countries seriously undermine Estonia's competitive position, then we must intervene. Thank you.

It is a great honour to be before you once again on the day of the celebration of your great democracy and to introduce you to the draft law amending the law on funded pensions and the law on state pension insurance and, in this connection, the law amending other laws and the Law on income tax. I apologise for the fact that Mr Jürgen Ligi cannot stand before you today and present this bill to you, but I will try to do my best to replace him. The draft has two very clear objectives, which can also be clearly seen here, because this draft is quite short. The first objective is very positive. The proposal is to increase the additional tax-free income on pensions from the current EUR 210 to EUR 220 per month. As we know, the overall tax-free income will be increased from EUR 144 to EUR 154 next year. Thus, the average pension will continue to be exempt from income tax with these two amendments, as from 1 January 2015, the draft and other amendments provide for tax-free income that does not exceed EUR 374 per month when we are talking about pension. The second proposal put before you in this draft relates to the parental pension system. The proposal is to postpone the entry into force of part of the parental pension until 1 January 2018. This concerns the part where the supplementary pension would be paid for raising a child born before 2013 at an additional rate of one year. If we look at why this needs to be done, we all know that the higher payments to the second pillar of the pension in 2014‐2018 will generate significant additional costs for the country. The second pillar of pensions is therefore, of course, growing. Next year, the additional cost of the second pillar will be over EUR 54 million and will rise to over EUR 60 million by 2017. This is the proposal of the Government of the Republic to the Grand Chamber of the Riigikogu. I believe that we can discuss this very comprehensive and long draft in a broad way, with the help of the issues that you have already prepared. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U916	Minister, thank you. No further questions. Remo Holsmer, member of the Finance Committee of the Riigikogu, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U917	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Monetary Affairs discussed this draft at two meetings: s. a on 9 and 12 June. The meetings were attended by Minister of Finance Jürgen Ligi, Head of the Department of Taxation Policy Lemmi Oro and Adviser Elo Haugas, as well as by Lidia Soontak, Head of the Social Security Department of the Ministry of Social Affairs, and an analyst from the Department of Social Policy Information and Analysis Age Viira. As the draft rapporteur told you about the content and objectives of the draft proposal, this draft is divided into two parts: the additional income tax exemption for pensioners will be increased from EUR 210 to EUR 220 and the entry into force of the remaining part of the parental pension will be postponed. I will give you an overview of the questions raised in the Commission and of the progress of the debate that has taken place. The Commission was asked about an additional exemption for pensioners in 2015. The additional tax exemption for pensioners, as I mentioned, will be increased to 220 euros. The Riigikogu also has a draft which increases the overall tax-free minimum to 154 euros. This total means that the estimated average old-age pension will remain exempt for EUR 374 next year. The second point of the draft act concerns the postponement of the outstanding part of the parental pension. The first and second phases of the system will remain in force. The members of the Commission asked whether the postponement would not have an adverse effect. This is described in the explanatory memorandum. The effect of deferring the pension supplement is small, up to 1% of the old-age pension. The question was asked why the risk of adverse effects on the social impact of the deferral of parental pension has been assessed as limited. The effects of the Act have been assessed in accordance with the Code of Conduct for the Evaluation of Impacts of the Good Regulation and the Regulatory Regulation. This guide states that the impact will be assessed as negative if the implementation of the draft is burdensome or restrictive for the target group. However, given that no pensioner has received this pension supplement under the parental pension scheme and that it is a predictable target group, no negative impact can be identified either. This is a postponement of the entry into force of the law, no substantive changes are made to the conditions and bases for the payment of the parental pension. The Commission was also asked why a pensioner would have to apply for income tax exemption if the law could be amended in this respect. According to the originator, the pensioner does not have to submit an application to calculate the additional tax-free income of pensioners. However, in order to calculate the general exemption, the application is necessary to ensure that the exemption is calculated only in one place, i.e. with one withholding agent. The Commission briefly discussed which countries in the European Union tax pensions. In response, we found that pensions are not taxed by Bulgaria, Lithuania, Slovakia and Hungary. Most EU countries tax pensions. I'm not gonna read that list here. If anyone's interested, I'm sure he'll get to know it. The impact on the budget of the postponement of entry into force was also discussed in the Commission. The originator estimates it to be EUR 86 million over three years. This is mainly linked to the time when the state will compensate ex post for the suspended payments to the second pillar of people's pensions during the crisis years. For information, the payments for the compensation of the second pillar amount to EUR 58 million this year, EUR 64 million next year, EUR 72 million in 2016 and EUR 79 million in 2017. Following the consultation, the Commission adopted the following decisions: It was decided to put the draft on the agenda by Tuesday 17 June (with 5 Commission members in favour, 3 against and no abstentions) with a proposal to terminate the first reading (with 5 Commission members in favour, 2 against and 1 abstention). The Commission proposes to set the deadline for the submission of amendments at 10 a.m. on 25 June (five Commission members in favour of this proposal, 3 against and no abstentions). Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U932	Let's move on to item six. The first reading of the draft Act on Child Protection initiated by the Government of the Republic begins. I ask the Minister of Social Affairs, Helmen Hunt, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U933	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I am very pleased to stand before you at first reading of the draft law on child protection. First of all, I would like to say congratulations and thanks. 2011/2015 of the IRL and Reform Party. The 2009 Action Plan included reviewing and updating the Child Protection Act, or the development of a new law, because the old law had fallen behind in time. I would like to thank Mr Pevkur and Mr Rõiva, because they were the Minister for Social Affairs when this bill was being developed, corrected and amended. Indeed, the Estonian Child Protection Act, which was in force for 22 years, which entered into force in 1993, has now lost its innovation, does not reflect the obligations arising from the requirements of life. What else can I say? Previously on "Good practices." In order to assess the impact of the implementation of the Act, an analysis of the effects of the draft RAKE Child Protection Act was commissioned from the University of Tartu Centre for Social Science Applied Studies and a fundamental analysis of the organisation of child protection was commissioned from PricewaterhouseCoopers. The basic analysis you have been able to read and see is 167 pages long. Now I'm talking about the bill. In short, the main keywords of the draft are: prevention and early notice; family and parents are the primary guarantors of child welfare; cross-cutting cooperation between all parties, such as education, health, law enforcement and welfare, but also precisely between different levels such as government, public authorities, municipalities. All this in order to ensure, in cooperation, the protection of children's rights and the support of the state to the local level through child protection activities. What else to talk about? The adoption of the Child Protection Act in the Riigikogu would be symbolic, which concerns everyone, because the draft makes the values of the entire society more child-friendly and more inclusive to children and families. Instead of dealing with the consequences, the draft will take the direction to prevent problems. It is important to assist children and their parents before problems arise (behavioural problems, mental disorders, child abuse, etc.). The draft provides for prevention as a principle that must be guided by all activities relating to children. At the same time, the state undertakes to give priority to prevention when planning its activities. What else? The development of prevention work is already underway, with three ministries developing and implementing a number of preventive activities in schools, in particular against school bullying, through a risky programme. The Parenthood Programme supports parents and enhances the capacity of local authorities in prevention. We have come to a stage where the explicit ban on child abuse and physical punishment is stated. The state is taking measures to support parents to provide teaching and guidance on how to deal with children without violence. International experience confirms that, for 35 years, punishment has been banned in Sweden, the Law on the Prohibition of Physical Punishment has also significantly reduced violence against children. Estonia is now entering the ranks of developed countries that value the child and its human rights. The draft specifies how to inform and assist a child in need of assistance. Since 2009, Estonia has been free of charge for children's help 116111. By calling this number, all people can report a child in distress 24 hours a day in Estonian, Russian or English. Where appropriate, the notifier may also remain anonymous. The important thing, though, is that helpers learn about any child in distress. The draft will for the first time create a national child protection organisation with a completely absent national supervision. In addition to the child protection that has been based on the local government, the national organisation together with support, such as counselling, assistance and training, supervision and other supporting activities of the local authorities' child protection workers. The national organisation of child welfare is set up at the existing system, the regional offices of the Social Insurance Board. They are as close as possible to the local government. The state will provide training for child protection workers, as previously mentioned, as well as work advice and supervisions, and will be ready to help with the development of local development plans with advice and strength. Thus, the local government receives real support for the organisation of complex child protection cases. The draft is accompanied by very comprehensive implementation plans and impact assessments. What else can I say? The draft states that the child protection worker is a social worker with a special education. This educational requirement will apply from 1 January 2020, i.e. people currently in child protection have time to improve and learn. For those working before the entry into force of the new version of the Child Protection Act as from 1 January 2016, the educational requirement will apply two years later, i.e. 2022. When the Chancellor of Justice audited and looked at the situation of the municipalities, it turned out that there were no child protection workers in every municipality, unfortunately. In order to have more of these employees, municipalities need to be advised and supported, as well as clarification of the need for a child protection worker. However, very many municipalities have made child welfare a priority. I very much hope that the debate on the draft law on child protection raises questions and debate. Kids are a priority for all of us. However, both the Chancellor of Justice, who is the children's ombudsman, and the Auditor General's audits have shown that a large proportion of children live in poverty. We are on the road to poverty alleviation and eradication when child benefits rise from 1 January 2015 and school lunch comes free of charge in upper secondary schools. I do not think there are any members of the Riigikogu in this Chamber who would oppose it. Thank you very much. I'm ready to answer questions.

The boy came back a little while later and cried. He couldn't find the rod because the branches were all too high, but he had a small stone in his hand. He gave this stone to his mother and told her to throw a stone at her because she couldn't find a rod. Then the mother and child sat down and both wept. Mother put this stone on the kitchen cabinet to remind her that it hurts when the child is either thrown with a stone or thrown with a rod. From then on, this family was no longer squeezing, but talking to the child. At the meeting of the social ministers in Stockholm, Queen Silvia, who came to this event, gave a very good example of Estonia becoming part of a country where the law explicitly states that children should not be physically punished. ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1055	The seventh item on the agenda is the first reading of the draft Act on Disability Allowances initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like to present the speech chair to Minister Urmas Kruuse!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1056	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Good guests! I am pleased to present you with the draft law on capacity support, which is part of a larger package of amendments, i.e. the reform of working capacity. With this reform, we want to support the desire and opportunity of people with health impairment to participate in the labour market, taking into account the needs of each individual. The bill will give the person the right and the opportunity to receive financial support from the unemployment fund and all the help needed to get a job. The principles of work capacity reform have been prepared in close cooperation with partners, whom I would certainly like to thank for the time spent and the contribution that has been made. Social protection schemes are being developed and modernised across Europe. People's behaviour and thinking patterns are changing and social protection schemes have to respond to these changes. With this draft, Estonia is moving closer to Europe with the modernisation of its social protection and is also modernising the scheme for determining incapacity for work. At present, we are basically retiring many people of working age and leaving them alone, they have to cope on their own. The new system, however, creates an individual approach; each individual is dealt with separately; its ability and desire to participate in society and the labour market are assessed. That is activation, or encouragement of man's participation. International experience confirms that only this approach is effective and helps people to cope better. The draft introduces three fundamental changes. Instead of loss of work capacity, the assessment of working capacity will take place. This shows what kind of work is suitable for a person, what kind of labour market measures he needs and what kind of services he is best supported by. Two, activity. The new scheme also provides support for people entering and staying in employment. This requires the participation of a person with partial capacity to work in the proposed measures. Third, the evaluation, activation and payment of support will be carried out by the Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund. The system will become clearer and simpler and a person with reduced capacity to work will receive all services from one place. Work capacity reform requires a change in the paradigm and thinking so far. An assessment of a person's ability, not his inability, will help him to find his place in society and participate in the labour market. The new assessment methodology has been developed with occupational doctors, which has been recognised by experts from the University of Tartu. It is also in line with the principles of assessment of working capacity in other European countries and is based on the recommendations of European medical and social security associations. The evaluation will determine whether a person is able to work, has partial or no capacity to work. The draft Employment Allowance Act creates a new type of allowance for work capacity. This allowance is granted to a person who has been identified as having a partial capacity to work or who does not have a capacity to work. The disability allowance is paid in two sizes: EUR 180 for a person with partial working capacity and EUR 320 for a person with no working capacity. From a certain level of income, support will be reduced. The capacity allowance shall be indexed each year. It is important to note that if a person has been found to have lost his or her capacity to work for at least two years and his or her current disability pension is higher than the benefit under the new system, he or she will be maintained at the level of the incapacity pension. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! Social services are an integral part of the labour capacity reform. The Government has approved a draft law amending the Social Welfare Act. It regulates, for example, the provision of rehabilitation services and the acquisition of tools so that people can go to work and keep their jobs. The Ministry of Social Affairs also allocates EUR 50 million to local governments in the new European Union financial period for the development of social services, including home care, EUR 7 million to enable the carers to go to work, EUR 12 million for disability transport, EUR 9 million for personal helper services, EUR 14 million for welfare services, for sign language interpreters, for rehabilitation, for the acquisition of aids, etc. Employers are motivated by a support package, ranging from the payment of social tax to the payment of wage subsidies and the adaptation of the workplace. In addition, under the Youth Guarantee, the unemployment fund will offer a first job measure aimed at reducing unemployment among young people, including those with disabilities. The unemployment fund also plans to offer a protected and supported job service. The reform will certainly increase the burden on the unemployment fund, as new cases are more complex and require more attention. It is likely that new customers will be damaged by health and have been away from working life for a long time. In this way, finding the best solutions is more time-consuming than it is now. The aim of the reform is also to build on the specific needs of each individual. But creating tailor-made solutions is more expensive. It is expensive to launch the reform, but the costs will start to decrease in the future. It is estimated that the cost of payment of disability pensions will decrease as of 2016. The Unemployment Insurance Fund needs additional staff for the administration of the evaluation of grants and the provision of services. These people are going to work mostly in county offices. The biggest addition is for case managers at a time when the new scheme has the largest number of entrants, while the exit has not yet started. The reform is expected to be implemented through the European Social Fund (ESF) funds from 2014 to 2020. Labour market measures, information systems and inter-agency cooperation, information and the development of an evaluation methodology are planned to be divided into a total amount of EUR 180 million, subject to the implementation of the reform. Labour market services are financed by the European Social Fund during a transitional period when the need for them is most intense. A further service financing scheme can be put in place once the first results of the reform and the actual needs of services and operating costs are visible. Members of the Riigikogu! The reform is comprehensive and affects the whole of society. One of the tasks of the labour capacity reform is to change the hardened prejudices, as if people with reduced working capacity were not full members of society, who have the right to find their place and also to contribute to the labour market if they wish and if possible. The draft is planned to enter into force as a law on 1 July 2015, when the new scheme will be applied to people who have not previously been identified as having lost their capacity to work. As of 1 January 2016, the current pensioners for incapacity for work will gradually enter the new scheme, as the period of incapacity for work identified arrives. Of course, it is difficult to plan major changes to the last detail; human behaviour and labour market developments are not always easily predictable. If the reform were to be postponed to a more distant future, it would not be fair to the people that the reform would help to find new opportunities and realise them. The draft has been drawn up following and taking into account the best practices in European countries in modernising the disability scheme. The Nordic countries, Holland, Great Britain, have served as an example. Work on the preparation of the draft has been under way for two years; this has been done with the parties involved in the reform. The development of the reform has been introduced to both the current and previous Social Committee of the Riigikogu. The reform is awaited and supported by the European Commission and the OECD, but above all by people who want to feel equal and inclusive in society. I ask the members of the Riigikogu to support the draft! Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1066	Thank you. Please, Urbo Vaarmann!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1067	Thank you, Mr President. Dear Urmas Kruuse! § 13 (3) of the draft regulation regulates the reduction of the capacity allowance if the person's income exceeds 60 times the current daily rate. If a person receives a gross salary of 640 euros during the year, but receives a bonus of 100 euros for Christmas, how much does the state punish him for this bonus? Would it be possible to find out the calculation that he will eventually have out of this EUR 100?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1068	Thank you. The good questioner is right that the aid will start to decrease from the moment the income exceeds EUR 640, but the last month will be taken into account. This means that if this limit has been exceeded last month, then next month the capacity allowance will be lower. What does this decrease mean? If we look at the range from EUR 640 to EUR 1005, the reduction is linked to a double coefficient. If the pay reaches the middle of this range, it can be said that the person's capacity to work allowance falls from EUR 180 to EUR 90.

Its capacity to work benefits will remain the same until the moment it is taken away, based on the fact that it has improved its state of health. His condition may remain the same or deteriorate, even so much that he basically becomes completely incapacitated. It's a place of choice. In essence, the activity requirement means that a person is not in a passive position as a beneficiary of a grant or pension in the present sense. If a person is currently receiving a pension, he or she usually has no obligation, may work and may not work. But now it's gonna change, and it's not gonna be like that anymore. When a person learns, he or she is definitely better socially connected to society, or some of his or her social quality indicators are better. But I don't really believe in the honorable questioner that people can study for 40 years without going to work.

Paragraph 3 provides for certain exceptions in which it is possible to assess the working capacity of younger or old-age workers. In Estonia, old-age pensioners are also known to be able to work. For what reason is this time limit provided for in paragraph 1? In the explanatory memorandum, I read about paragraph 3, which describes the exceptions that arise from specific laws. But perhaps you will explain, which is why this right is provided for until the age of retirement and why shouldn't pensioners also have this right?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1192	Thank you, Reverend Asker! This work capacity reform, in its objective content as a whole, concerns the working age population. I also said before about the disability allowance that if a person reaches retirement age and his or her pension is higher than the disability allowance, he or she will receive his or her pension. Older people also have additional retraining opportunities. Let us look at the reform as a whole and at the possible measures of the European Union. We have also proposed measures with a common denominator of 55+. If we look at the labour market, we can't hide our heads in the sand. The ability of people approaching retirement age to participate in the labour market may also be reduced for one reason or another, without re-training or activity requirements, and may fall out of the labour market. At the age of 59, these people are going out of the labour market. All the work hands are necessary, but we did not think that we should impose activity requirements beyond the so-called retirement age.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1215	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The new day is now, and it is time to introduce the activities of the Social Affairs Committee. The Social Affairs Committee discussed the Employment Capacity Allowance Act at five regular sessions and also at another extraordinary session. Two of these five ordinary sittings were open to the public, while the other discussions were common. The first meeting was held on 3 June. The Social Affairs Committee heard about the impact of Saar Poll's study on the Employment Capacity Allowance Act. We were briefed on the survey and highlighted a number of important things. It was stressed that cooperation with employers is essential in the implementation of the labour capacity reform and that attention should be paid to the provision of jobs away from the centres. It was proposed to identify the capacity of local authorities to create jobs, etc. The members of the Social Committee listened to the summary, asked questions and took note of the results of the study. The next meeting, which took place on 9 June, prepared the first reading of the draft Employment Capacity Allowance Act. There were the Minister and the employees of the Ministry of Social Affairs who introduced us to this draft. The Minister made a thorough review of the draft law. He pointed out that the draft would allow for the improvement of people's working environment and other important things that are important for employers as well as for people who have lost their ability to work. The Minister also stressed that the Employment Capacity Allowance Act aims to employ 15000 persons with partial working capacity over the next few years and that we have EUR 180 million of ESF money to implement the reform. According to the Minister, he supports sending the draft to first reading. At the same meeting, the Minister and the officials of the Ministry of Social Affairs were also asked questions. The most important ones have already sounded in this hall. For example, there was one question about the recruitment of 500 officials to the Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund, to which the answer was that in fact officials are not recruited, but case managers, specialists, accountants, because the handling of cases, as well as the use of European Social Fund funds, is more labour intensive than the normal use of state budget resources. There were other important questions, but most of them were also submitted to the Minister in this Chamber today, so I will not go over them. The next meeting was held on 10 June. It was a public hearing held in the conference room. The purpose of this meeting was to hear the representatives of the target groups of the Employment Capacity Support Act, as well as the representatives of the institutions involved in the implementation of this reform. Representatives of the unemployment fund, Lecturer of the Social Labour Institute of TLU, representatives of the Confederation of Employers, as well as representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs, participated in the meeting. The representatives of the target groups were representatives of the Deaf Association, the Development Centre Avitus, the Careers' Association, the Association of Persons with reduced mobility, the Union of the blind and the Deaf Youth Organisation, as well as, of course, representatives of the Chamber of People with Disabilities. The views of all participants on the draft and their recommendations were heard at this meeting. The House of People with Disabilities made very clear proposals for this session, which highlighted some of the weaknesses of the draft and put forward proposals that the Committee on Social Affairs can discuss in its future work. On 12 June, another meeting took place in which procedural decisions were taken. The fifth meeting was on 16 June, Monday. It was once again a public session in the conference room, where the views of the target groups, the unemployment fund and the Ministry of Social Affairs, as well as the representatives of the Social Insurance Board on the Employment Capacity Allowance Act, were once again heard. An extraordinary meeting was held today, where the Committee on Social Affairs specified the deadline for tabling amendments and set out its summer work schedule. Finally, the procedural decisions adopted by the Committee on Social Affairs on the draft law on capacity benefit for work. The first proposal was to put the draft law on the plenary agenda by 17 June (it was yesterday or now the day before yesterday, the two days have passed). It was proposed that the first reading be closed and that the deadline for tabling amendments be set at 10 a.m. on 11 August and that I be appointed rapporteur. All these decisions were taken by the Commission in a consensual manner. That's all.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1219	Dear Chairman, Dear coworkers and good friends on the balcony, who may not be interested in the reform of working capacity, even though the subject is very important! Work capacity reform has been prepared for a long time. This is a major and fundamental reform with a very noble aim. I think that there is no political party or organisation of people with special needs doubting this objective. The aim is to help people with special needs according to their abilities and opportunities in the labour market. This objective is noble and must be achieved. At the same time, when we look at the opposition of the organisations of people with disabilities, people with special needs, when we see that employers' organisations are also very sceptical and, six months ago, the trade unions who have recently fallen a little bit quieter spoke very sceptically about this draft, it can be said that this 360 million euros, or the reform that is going to cost nearly six billion kroons in old Estonian money, is poorly prepared. If opposition is so great, we cannot be satisfied with the explanation that everyone, except the government, has misunderstood the matter. It's all got its back bottom. The former Minister of Social Affairs, who has become Prime Minister, has publicly stated that this reform is his child and must be carried out. At the same time, there is at least one minister in the government, Helmen Kütt, Minister of Social Affairs, who is directly responsible for organising the daily lives of people with disabilities, very clearly and openly, and also said that this reform cannot be implemented in this form. What is missing in this reform? It is precisely half, or even more, what is missing that will help people with disabilities into the labour market. We are talking about social welfare services, which are largely provided by municipalities. We're talking about a rehabilitation system that's out of date. We are talking about the organization of a system of aids that does not meet today's requirements. We are also talking about an educational system for people with disabilities that is not up to date. I do not agree with the Minister's view that we cannot describe and solve all the social problems that people with special needs have to face. Unfortunately, or fortunately, it is precisely during the implementation of the labour capacity reform that we must also talk about all other problems, because it is only by implementing the labour capacity reform that those people with the same special needs will not actually be able to enter the labour market. It is therefore obvious that this reform has been prepared poorly. I would not say that this has been done in haste because it has been prepared for at least two years. It is not true that the rhetoric is that how wonderful we have democracy, the government has given the labour force reform to the Riigikogu to discuss, and it will be done in the Riigikogu. This competence, which can actually prepare a complex solution, is within the government. It is the task of the Government and the Ministry of Social Affairs, together with other ministries, to prepare this reform. Currently, the Riigikogu has come with a crude and unthinkable solution, which is actually not discussed with the organisations of people with special needs and where their proposals have not been taken into account. It is not possible to patch up this draft with a few proposals; there is just half of the work missing. The Riigikogu is not such a place as the Toots School that half the work is good. People with special needs need need a holistic solution, because they are the ones who are not sitting here today in this warm room, but their lives will be arranged in the future. There are also other issues related to long-term commitments. As I said, this reform will cost a total of EUR 360 million. Meelis Paavel, Head of the Unemployment Insurance Fund, has also stated in the Commission that the current current expenditure of the Unemployment Insurance Fund, which is EUR 34 million per year, will rise to EUR 67 million by 2020. The Minister also acknowledged that there is not yet a solution to what kind of money will be used to cover these expenditures in 2020, when Europe's support ends. These options are set out in the explanatory memorandum: state budget expenditure, unemployment fund expenditure or combined costs. But it is, in fact, a very big commitment to the state, and yet there is no answer to that question. One important group of people excluded from this employment programme is very directly involved with people with disabilities. Those are the caretakers. In fact, the government should withdraw this bill and think about how to bring into the labour market healthy people who are excluded from the labour market because they are forced to stay at home and care for their loved ones due to a lack of care services. It's the easiest way to get them there. Improving their situation could be part of the employment reform. But one thing is clear: more than 500 new jobs will be created in the unemployment fund. This is perhaps the only clear number of jobs the government can create for this EUR 360 million. Unfortunately, these jobs are not for people with special needs, but they are for officials, no matter what we call them. It is a pity that we miss the opportunity to complete a comprehensive reform with which we would also include social welfare services. We now come to political decisions. I want to turn to the Social Democrats. If you don't trust your minister, I don't know who you trust. If you don't support him, I have a question why you have such a minister in the government. Minister Helmen Kütt is perhaps the most competent person today to speak in this area. His views are even perhaps better known in the group than in the government and in our committee. I urge you very seriously, before the vote that comes today, to consider whether you trust and support your minister or not. I know that you have had very serious discussions on this subject. Finally, I would like to say that the government should actually withdraw this draft itself and come here with a peacefully thought-out and disability-coordinated proposal. That is why the Group of the Fatherland and Res Publica Union is proposing to reject the draft Employment Capacity Allowance Act 678. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1223	Ladies and gentlemen, Mr. Minister! We cannot support an extremely expensive bill that raises officials, pushes up acute issues from the state to the unemployment fund and municipalities and makes a disabled person unemployed. On behalf of the group, I will put forward a motion for rejection of the draft. You don't need any more paper, do you?

On this basis, it is considered that one case manager deals with about 100 cases (80 or 120 cases in the customer portfolio). The degree of complexity of the cases also leads to the development of a client portfolio. So it's based on practical experience. The case manager must definitely start going out of the house more, interacting with networks, local authorities and non-profit institutions that are all involved in this system.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1250	Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1251	Sure. Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1252	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! In a country with an ageing and declining population, the number of dependants of every employed person is inevitably increasing. However, if the taxpayers' ranks dwindle, society will not be able to guarantee a decent life from their taxes to either old age or inability to work. It is therefore in the interest of all of us to create a situation in which as many people as possible can find appropriate applications. We have almost 100,000 disability pensions, one third of them working, two thirds not. Around 40,000 of them, so say polls, would be happy to work for themselves, want to participate in society and earn a decent salary if they only had opportunities and jobs. It is not just a matter of living standards; it is also a question of human dignity. Work capacity reform has been launched to help as many people as possible to work with complex and flexible measures. The most important value of this reform can be considered to be a change in mentality. People with partial work ability have said that many would like to be applied appropriately. They do not dispute the need for a labour capacity reform. However, they have said that problems need to be identified and started again. We are talking about really big restructuring, which is endlessly linked to education, health services and jobs, including part-time jobs, as well as the accident and occupational disease insurance law. In some cases, a very legitimate criticism of the relevant stakeholders has gone to the wider arch and often to the associated areas. I dare confirm that the Social Democrats agree that no law on labour capacity reform will be adopted rashly. There is still a lot of work to be done on the drafts to ensure that the work capacity reform fulfils its central objective of helping as many people as possible to work. The reform must be made so that those for whom it has been made can benefit from it. This will require further in-depth discussions. Hopefully, the whole picture will clear up by autumn. During the discussion of this draft, a number of additional problems have been clearly drawn up, which are not directly related to the draft, but certainly need to be resolved. Close cooperation with stakeholders is needed. I consider it necessary to conclude a cooperation agreement on goodwill to solve the issues of people with health impairment. I have proposed to the Minister for Social Affairs and the Minister for Labour and Health that work be done to bring about an agreement that will identify the issues and points of pain in the field and which will set out the actions needed to achieve the objectives, together with deadlines and those responsible, so that they can continue to be guided. Social Democrats consider the availability of suitable jobs for people with special needs as a key element of the reform. The government should draw up a plan for creating new jobs, including part-time jobs, and provide employers with additional levers to be interested in hiring people with partial employment capacity. The country with the unemployment fund is precisely the one who should show the way by recruiting people with reduced capacity to act as case managers and consultants. After labour market training, occupational rehabilitation and, if necessary, the appointment of a support person, a person with less working capacity must still be able to work. That's literally what I'm saying. To this end, the issue of transport needs to be resolved so that people with special needs can get to work from a distance, the procedure for paying transport support needs to be updated. Social Democrats also see the need for differentiation of work capacity allowances so that people with greater loss of work capacity will receive more support, people with less work capacity could receive somewhat less support. Clearly too low is the limit in the current version of the draft, according to which people with a gross monthly fee of more than EUR 640 will be reduced. This ceiling should be set significantly higher, for example by the average gross wage. The aim is to help as many people as possible to work, but there must be young people at the heart of restructuring. If a young person with partial employment capacity completes a vocational school or receives other training, he or she usually does not enter the unemployment fund's interest. He must be directed there as soon as he has completed his education if he has not received a job, because if he stays home or in a nursing home for years, the skills he has learned will be forgotten and any interest in finding a job will be lost. Yet, he could be faced with a long and meaningful working life. With regard to the Social Democrats' amendments, which are largely based on the opinion of stakeholders, we certainly intend to negotiate and calculate their impact. I hope that we can discuss these and other opinions and proposals at the meetings of the Committee on Social Affairs. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1253	Thank you. I ask Jüri Jaansoni, representative of the Estonian Reform Party Group, to the Riigikogu Chair of Speech!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-06-18_U1254	Ladies and gentlemen, Work capacity reform has been built piece by piece for a long time, since 2010. Of course, this whole system is not ready for now. We are discussing today the core part of this reform, the draft law on capacity support for work, which is going to reverse completely the entire system of incapacity for work. The main objective of the employment capacity reform is to give people who are excluded from society, who have permanent employment capacity, the opportunity to find some kind of appropriate output in society, whether it is a job or an education. The involvement of these people is also necessary for society, not just for these people themselves. In this context, I find it inappropriate to talk about any problems that may arise in 2020 and to ask questions as to whether we will have enough money or what will happen next. Is there a chance that in 2020 we will not have enough money for one part of society for taxpayers? I don't see a chance like that. We're talking about employing 500 case managers to get 15,000 people to work. I think the proportions are completely in place here. We are talking about a very long-term system, basically one that should help these people in a positive key to infinity. The work capacity reform consists of its core, which we are currently discussing, and of the many support systems that we are gradually developing and building up as a whole. In the Committee on Social Affairs, we have already opened a debate on amendments to the Social Welfare Act. In the autumn, we will start reading all the draft laws on these topics. In presenting the impact study on the employment capacity reform, Saar Poll also made it clear that they do not see the possibility of the core part of the reform and the supporting elements being dealt with together at first, because these things will just get very mixed up. It is important that the core section is currently being dealt with, that the support systems then gradually come back. I would also like to stress that none of the representatives of the target group, who have been very much discussed here today, have claimed that there is no need for a reform of working capacity. On the contrary, many of them have said that the sooner the work capacity reform is implemented, the better for them. Good ambassadors! I, too, call on you all to vote in favour of the completion of the first reading of the draft Employment Allowance Act. This is for the simple reason that the Social Affairs Committee has a long summer to discuss all the amendments in a calm manner in order to be prepared in the autumn to process and process draft supporting laws. Thank you.
